. Note that any subset of edges
H\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}
induces a subgraph
\mathsf{H}\subseteq{\cal K}_{n}
containing no isolated vertices. In fact, there is a one-to-one correspondence between subgraphs without isolated vertices and subsets of edges. We therefore identify each character
\chi_{H}
with a subgraph
\mathsf{H}
and denote it by
\chi_{\mathsf{H}}
. Observe that
\chi_{\mathsf{H}}
is polynomial in the entries of
\mathsf{G}
, with degree
|e(\mathsf{H})|
, which, with slight abuse of notation, we also denote by
|\mathsf{H}|
. It is well known (and easy to verify) that the set
\left\{\chi_{\mathsf{H}}\right\}_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}
forms an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}({\cal H}_{0})
. Hence, by Parseval’s identity we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}\right]=\left\|\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\left|\left\langle\chi_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G}),\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})\right\rangle\right|^{2}.

(94)

Building on (
94
), let us give two equivalent characterizations for
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}\right]
.

Proposition 1
.

Consider the setting in Section
2
, and denote
\lambda^{2}\triangleq\chi^{2}(p||q)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma\right]

(95)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathsf{H}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}\right]

(96)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|}\right],

(97)

where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a fixed arbitrary copy of
\Gamma
in
{\cal K}_{n}
, the probability in (
96
) is taken w.r.t. a random copy
\mathsf{H}
of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
, the probability and expectation in (
95
) and (
97
) are taken w.r.t. a random copy
\Gamma\sim\mathsf{Unif}({\cal S}_{\Gamma})
, the summation is over subgraphs containing no isolated vertices, and
|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|
is the number edges in the intersection of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

In order to prove Proposition
1
, we will need two technical results. We start with the following definition.

Definition 3
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a subgraph of
{\cal K}_{n}
, and
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
be a subgraph of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Define
{\cal N}(\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})
as the number of copies of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
in
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and
{\cal M}(\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})
as the number of copies of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
which contain
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 2
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a subgraph of
{\cal K}_{n}
, and
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
be a subgraph. Let
\mathsf{H}\sim\mathsf{Unif}({\cal S}_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}})
be a uniform random copy of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
, and
let
\Gamma
be a uniform random copy of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Then,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathsf{H}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}\right]=\frac{{\cal N}(\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})}{\left|{\cal S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right|}=\frac{{\cal M}(\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})}{|{\cal S}_{\Gamma}|}={\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma\right].

(98)

Proof.

The first and third equalities follow directly from the definition of the distribution of a random copy. It remains to show that

\displaystyle{\cal N}(\mathsf{H^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime})\cdot|{\cal S}_{\Gamma}|={\cal M}(\mathsf{H^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime})\cdot|{\cal S}_{\mathsf{H}}|,

(99)

which follows from the observation that the terms on both sides of (
99
) represent the cardinality of the set,

\displaystyle\left\{\left.(\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime})\right|\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\cal K}_{n},\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\cong\Gamma^{\prime}\right\}.

(100)

∎

Thus, Lemma
2
establishes the second equality in (
96
). Next, we prove the third equality in (
97
) through the following lemma. In fact, we derive a slightly more general identity, which will prove useful in establishing (
\mathsf{Arg}_{1}
), described in Subsection
5.1
.

Lemma 3
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two arbitrary (perhaps different) subgraphs of
{\cal K}_{n}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(101)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right],

(102)

where
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
is a fixed arbitrary copy of
\Gamma_{1}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
, the summation is over graphs containing no isolated vertices, and the probabilities and the expectation are taken w.r.t. two independent random copies
\Gamma_{i}\sim\mathsf{Unif}({\cal S}_{\Gamma_{i}})
, for
i=1,2
.

Proof of Lemma
3
.

Since we deal with graphs containing no isolated vertices, we slightly abuse notation by treating a graph
\mathsf{H}
as a subset of edges and denote
|\mathsf{H}|\triangleq|e(\mathsf{H})|
. Now, let us analyze the r.h.s. of (
101
), beginning with the following chain of equalities,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1},\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(103)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]

(104)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]

(105)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}

(106)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]\sum_{i=0}^{\left|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\right|}\binom{|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}|}{i}\lambda^{2i}

(107)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]\cdot\left(1+\lambda^{2}\right)^{\left|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\right|}

(108)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right],

(109)

which proves the first equality in (
101
). For the second equality in (
102
), recall that by Lemma
2
, for any subgraph
\mathsf{H}
of a fixed copy
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
of
\Gamma_{1}
, we have that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\right]=\frac{{\cal M}(\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{1}^{\prime})}{|{\cal S}_{\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}|}=\frac{{\cal N}(\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{1}^{\prime})}{|{\cal S}_{\mathsf{H}}|}.

(110)

By symmetry, the above expression is invariant under isomorphisms. Namely, for any
\mathsf{H^{\prime}}\subseteq{\cal K}_{n}
, if
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to some subgraph
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\right]=\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H^{\prime}}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\right].

(111)

If
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
is not isomorphic to any subgraph of
\Gamma_{1}
, then the probability above is clearly zero. We define an equivalence relation on subgraphs of
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
, where two subgraphs are considered equivalent if and only if they are isomorphic. Let
[\mathsf{H}]
denote the equivalence class of a subgraph
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}
, and let
{\cal P}
be the set of all equivalence classes. Then, using (
110
) and the fact that
|[\mathsf{H}]|={\cal N}(\mathsf{H},\Gamma)
, we may write,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\cong\mathsf{H}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(112)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\cong\mathsf{H}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(113)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}}|{\cal S}_{\mathsf{H}}|\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(114)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\cal N}(\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{1})\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(115)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}}\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\in[\mathsf{H}]}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(116)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right].

(117)

which completes the proof.
∎

We are now in a position to complete the proof of Proposition
1
.

Proof of Proposition
1
.

We recall (
94
) and proceed by computing the Fourier coefficients. From the definition of
\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})
in (
88
), it follows that for any character
\chi_{\mathsf{H}}
, we have,

\displaystyle\left\langle\chi_{\mathsf{H}},\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})\right\rangle_{{\cal H}_{0}}=\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\chi_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G})\cdot\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}\left[\chi_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G})\right].

(118)

Let us compute
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}[\chi_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G})]
, for each
\mathsf{H}
. We have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}\left[\chi_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}|\Gamma}\left[\chi_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G})\right]\right]

(119)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}|\Gamma}\left[\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathsf{H}}\frac{\mathsf{G}_{ij}-q}{\sqrt{q(1-q)}}\right]\right]

(120)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathsf{H}}\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}|\Gamma}\left[\frac{\mathsf{G}_{ij}-q}{\sqrt{q(1-q)}}\right]\right],

(121)

where we have used the fact that conditioned on
\Gamma
, the edges of
\mathsf{G}
are independent. Depending on the edge
\{i,j\}
and its relation to
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{G}
, there are two possible cases.

•

If
\{i,j\}\in\mathsf{H}
is such that
\{i,j\}\in e(\Gamma)
, then
\mathsf{G}_{ij}\sim\mathsf{Bern}(p)
, and accordingly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\frac{\mathsf{G}_{ij}-q}{\sqrt{q(1-q)}}\right|\{i,j\}\in e(\Gamma)\right]=p\sqrt{\frac{1-q}{q}}-(1-p)\sqrt{\frac{q}{1-q}}=\frac{p-q}{\sqrt{q(1-q)}}\triangleq\lambda,

(122)

and we note that
\lambda=\sqrt{\chi^{2}(p||q)}
.

•

If
\{i,j\}\in\mathsf{H}
is such that
\{i,j\}\not\in e(\Gamma)
, then
\mathsf{G}_{ij}\sim\mathsf{Bern}(q)
, and accordingly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\frac{\mathsf{G}_{ij}-q}{\sqrt{q(1-q)}}\right|\{i,j\}\not\in e(\Gamma)\right]=0.

(123)

Combining the above results, we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}|\Gamma}\left[\frac{\mathsf{G}_{ij}-q}{\sqrt{q(1-q)}}\right]=\lambda\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\{i,j\}\in\mathsf{H}\cap e(\Gamma)\right\},

(124)

which in turn implies that,

\displaystyle\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathsf{H}}\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}|\Gamma}\left[\frac{\mathsf{G}_{ij}-q}{\sqrt{q(1-q)}}\right]=\lambda^{|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma\right\}}.

(125)

Substituting in (
121
) we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}\left[\chi_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[\lambda^{|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{\mathsf{H}\subseteq e(\Gamma)\right\}}\right]=\lambda^{|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma\right].

(126)

Therefore, inserting (
126
) in (
94
), we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}\left[\chi_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{G})\right]^{2}

(127)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma\right]^{2}.

(128)

This proves the first equality in (
97
). The third equality in (
97
) follows by setting
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma
in Lemma
3
, and using the fact that, by symmetry, we can always consider
\Gamma_{2}
as a fixed copy instead of a uniform random copy, while
\Gamma_{1}
remains uniform and random.
∎

5.3
The dense regime

Following the outline in Subsection
5.1
, in this subsection, we consider the dense regime, where
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(1)
(in particular,
\delta<p-q<1-\delta
, for some
\delta>0
), and provide a complete formal proof for the statistical lower bounds in Theorem
7
. We will generalize this proof strategy in Subsection
5.4
to other regimes.

Theorem 7
.

Assume that
\lambda^{2}=\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(1)
, and that
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
is any sequence of subgraphs such that
|v(\Gamma)|\leq(1-\delta)n
, for some fixed
\delta>0
.

1.

For
\mu(\Gamma)\geq\alpha\log|v(\Gamma)|
, weak detection is impossible if

\displaystyle\mu(\Gamma)\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)\alpha}{2+\alpha\log(1+\lambda^{2})}\log n,

(129)

for some
\varepsilon>0
, and strong detection is possible if

\displaystyle\mu(\Gamma)\geq(1+\varepsilon)\frac{\log n}{d_{\mathsf{KL}}(p||q)},

(130)

for some
\varepsilon>0
.

2.

For
\mu(\Gamma)=o\left(\log|v(\Gamma)|\right)
, strong detection is impossible if

\displaystyle\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|,d_{\max}^{2}(\Gamma)\right)\leq n^{1-\varepsilon},

(131)

for some
\varepsilon>0
, and strong detection is possible if

\displaystyle\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|,d_{\max}^{2}(\Gamma)\right)\geq n^{1+f(n)},

(132)

where
f(n)
is an
o(1)
function.

The above result asserts that if
\Gamma
has super-logarithmic density, i.e.,
\mu(\Gamma)=\Omega(\log|v(\Gamma)|)
, then the statistical barrier is determined by
\mu(\Gamma)
only. On the other hand, if
\Gamma
has sub-logarithmic density, the statistical barrier is determined by
|e(\Gamma)|
and
d_{\max}(\Gamma)
. As mentioned in Subsection
5.1
, our proof consists of three main steps, as detailed below. Before we continue, we introduce the following two definitions, which play a significant role in our analysis.

Definition 4

(Vertex cover)
.

Let
\mathsf{G}=(\mathsf{V},\mathsf{E})
be an undirected graph. A set
\mathsf{U}\subseteq\mathsf{V}
is called a vertex cover of
\mathsf{G}
if any edge in
\mathsf{E}
has a vertex in
\mathsf{U}
. We define the vertex cover number
\tau(\mathsf{G})
of
\mathsf{G}
as the minimal cardinality vertex cover set in
\mathsf{G}
.

Definition 5
.

A sequence of graphs
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
is called vertex cover-degree balanced, or simply
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced, if

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\left(\tau(\Gamma)\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)\right)}{\log|e(\Gamma)|}=1.

(133)

5.3.1
Combinatorial bound through vertex covers

In this subsection, we prove the impossibility result in Theorem
6
. To that end, as mentioned in the previous subsection, it suffices to upper bound
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}]
.
Throughout this subsection, for the benefit of readability, we let
k\triangleq|v(\Gamma)|
,
d\triangleq d_{\max}(\Gamma)
,
\tau\triangleq\tau(\Gamma)
, and
\mu\triangleq\mu(\Gamma)
. In light of Proposition
1
, our goal is to bound
{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma}[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma]
(or, equivalently,
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathsf{H}}[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}]
). The following result shows that, from the perspective of dependence on graph-theoretic properties of
\Gamma
, this probability can be upper bounded in terms of
(d_{\max}(\Gamma),\tau(\Gamma))
alone.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a fixed copy of
\Gamma
,
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
be a subgraph containing no isolated vertices, with
\ell
vertices and
m
connected components, and
\mathsf{H}
a random copy of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
. Then,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma\right]={\mathbb{P}}_{\mathsf{H}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}\right]\leq\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}\triangleq\vartheta(m,\ell).

(134)

For the proof of Lemma
4
we will need the following important observation.

Observation 1
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a graph with
\ell\leq n
vertices enumerated as
v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}
and let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{\ell})
be distributed as

X_{1}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left([n]\right),\quad\text{and}\quad X_{i}|X_{1},\dots X_{i-1}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left([n]\setminus\{X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\}\right),

for all
1<i<\ell
. Then, the random graph
\mathsf{H_{U}}=\left(v({\mathsf{H_{U}}}),E({\mathsf{H_{U}}})\right)
with

V_{\mathsf{U}}=\{X_{1},\dots X_{\ell}\},\quad\text{and}\quad E_{\mathsf{U}}=\{\left.\left(X_{i},X_{j}\right)\right|(v_{i},v_{j})\in\mathsf{H}\},

is a uniform random copy of
\mathsf{H}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
.

5.3.2
Reduction to the vertex cover-degree balanced case

There is a gap between the lower bound in Theorem
6
and the upper bounds in Theorem
5
, which closes if
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced. As discussed in Subsection
5.1
, we address this gap and prove that Theorem
5
is tight through two main arguments: 1) We show that if
\Gamma
can be decomposed into a set of
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced graphs, then detection is impossible as long as each subgraph in the decomposition is undetectable, and 2) we construct such a decomposition. In this subsection, we focus on the first argument. Consider the subsequent definition.

Definition 8
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be simple graph containing no isolated vertices. We say that
G
is decomposed into a set of
M
edge-disjoint graphs
\{G_{\ell}=(V_{\ell},E_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{M}
, and denote
G=\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}G_{i}
, if
E
is the disjoint union of the sets
E_{1},\dots,E_{M}
, and for all
\ell
we have
V_{\ell}\subseteq V
.

The following is the main result of this subsection.

Proposition 2
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of graphs, and assume that each can be decomposed into a set of edge-disjoint graphs, i.e.,
\Gamma=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{M}\Gamma_{\ell}
. Then,
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}]=O(1)
if

\displaystyle\max_{\ell=1,\dots,M}\left(\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu(\Gamma_{\ell})}}{n-|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell}),d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})^{2}\right)\right)\leq C,

(138)

where
\lambda_{M}^{2}\triangleq(1+\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}}-1
, and
C>0
is a universal constant.

To prove Proposition
2
we need the following generalization of Theorem
6
, which provides the conditions under which the moment generating function of the intersection of
two arbitrary
subgraphs is bounded.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}=(\Gamma_{1,n})_{n}
and
\Gamma_{2}=(\Gamma_{2,n})_{n}
be two sequences of graphs. If,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{\min(\mu(\Gamma_{1}),\mu(\Gamma_{2}))}}{n-\min\left(|v(\Gamma_{1})|,|v(\Gamma_{2})|\right)}\max\left(\sqrt{\tau(\Gamma_{1})\tau(\Gamma_{2})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{1})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{2})},d_{\max}(\Gamma_{1})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{2})\right)\leq C,

(139)

for some
C>0
, then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]=O(1),

(140)

where the expectation is taken w.r.t.
\Gamma_{2}\sim\mathsf{Unif}({\cal S}_{\Gamma_{2}})
and
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
is fixed copy of
\Gamma_{1}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
.

Proof of Lemma
7
.

Throughout this proof, we denote
\tau_{i}=\tau(\Gamma_{i})
,
d_{i}=d_{\max}(\Gamma_{i})
,
\mu_{i}=\mu(\Gamma_{i})
, and
k_{i}=|v(\Gamma_{i})|
, for
i=1,2
. The proof follows by generalizing the arguments in the proof of Theorem
6
. Specifically, recall that by Lemma
3
we have (by symmetry we can take
\Gamma_{1}
to be a fixed copy
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right].

(141)

As in the proof of Theorem
6
, we use Lemma
4
to upper bound the probability
{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}]
, which in turn implies that the summand in (
141
) depends on
\mathsf{H}
through
(|v(\mathsf{H})|,|e(\mathsf{H})|,m(\mathsf{H}))
only. Note that any subgraph
\mathsf{H}
of
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
for which the probability
{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}]
is non-zero, must be isomorphic to a subgraph of
\Gamma_{2}
. Thus, the number of vertices and edges in
\mathsf{H}
cannot exceed
\min(k_{1},k_{2})=\bar{k}
and
|v(\mathsf{H})|\cdot\min(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\triangleq|v(\mathsf{H})|\cdot\bar{\mu}
, respectively. Denote by
{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}(\Gamma_{1})
the number of subgraphs of
\Gamma_{1}
with exactly
m
connected components,
\ell
vertices, and
j
edges. Using Lemma
4
and
, we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]

(142)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}]

(143)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\left\lfloor\ell\bar{\mu}\right\rfloor}|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot\lambda^{2j}\cdot\frac{(2\tau_{2})^{m}d_{2}^{\ell-m}}{(n-{\bar{k}})^{\ell}}

(144)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\frac{(2\tau_{2})^{m}d_{2}^{\ell-m}}{(n-{\bar{k}})^{\ell}}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\left\lfloor\ell\bar{\mu}\right\rfloor}\lambda^{2j}\cdot\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\tau_{1}^{m}\cdot(ed_{1})^{\ell-m}\binom{\left\lfloor\ell\bar{\mu}\right\rfloor}{j}

(145)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}}\cdot ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{m}.

(146)

On one hand, if,

\displaystyle\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\geq 1+\alpha,

(147)

for some
\alpha>0
, then the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
146
) is dominated by
\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{\ell/2}
, and accordingly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}}\cdot\sqrt{2e\cdot\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\cdot d_{1}d_{2}}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\right)^{\ell},

(148)

which is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}}\cdot\sqrt{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\cdot d_{1}d_{2}}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\leq C,

(149)

for a sufficiently small universal constant
C>0
. On the other hand, if,

\displaystyle\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\leq 1+o(1),

(150)

then the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
146
) is dominated by
(1+\varepsilon)^{\ell}
, and accordingly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}}\cdot ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\right)^{\ell},

(151)

which is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}}\cdot d_{1}d_{2}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\leq C,

(152)

for a sufficiently small constant universal
C
. Finally, we note that (
147
) holds exactly when (
149
) dominates (
152
) (perhaps, up to a multiplicative constant factor). Thus, the condition in (
139
) is sufficient for (
140
) to hold.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
2
.

Proof of Proposition
2
.

For any
1\leq\ell\leq M
, we denote
k_{\ell}\triangleq|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|
,
d_{\ell}\triangleq d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})
,
\mu_{\ell}\triangleq\mu(\Gamma_{\ell})
, and
\tau_{\ell}\triangleq\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})
. Below, we first assume that
M
is fixed, independent with
n
, and treat the case where
M=M_{n}
. Using Proposition
1
and Hölder’s inequality, we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|}\right]

(153)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bigcup_{i,j=1}^{M}|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma^{\prime}_{j})|}\right]

(154)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[\prod_{i,j=1}^{M}(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma^{\prime}_{j})|}\right]

(155)

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i,j=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}^{\frac{1}{M^{2}}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}\cdot|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma^{\prime}_{j})|}\right]

(156)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{i}}^{\frac{1}{M^{2}}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}\cdot|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma^{\prime}_{j})|}\right]

(157)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{i}}^{\frac{1}{M^{2}}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2}_{M})^{|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma^{\prime}_{j})|}\right],

(158)

where for all
i,j\in[M]
,
\Gamma^{\prime}_{j}
is a fixed copy of
\Gamma_{j}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
(induced by the fixed copy
\Gamma^{\prime}
), and the expectation in (
157
) and (
158
) is taken w.r.t. a uniformly distributed random copy of
\Gamma_{i}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
. Let
C>0
be the smallest constant for which Theorem
6
and Lemma
7
hold simultaneously. For
i=j
, by Theorem
6
, we know that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{i})|}\right]=O(1),

(159)

provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu_{i}}\max(\tau_{i}d_{i},d_{i}^{2})}{n-k_{i}}\leq C.

(160)

This, however, is satisfied when (
138
) holds because,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu_{i}}\max(\tau_{i}d_{i},d_{i}^{2})}{n-k_{i}}\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq M}{\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu_{j}}\max(\tau_{j}d_{j},d_{j}^{2})}{n-k_{j}}}\leq C.

(161)

Next, for
i\neq j
, we note that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\min(\mu_{i},\mu_{j})}\max(\sqrt{\tau_{i}\tau_{j}d_{i}d_{j}},d_{i}d_{j})}{n-\min(k_{i},k_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\min(\mu_{i},\mu_{j})}\max(\sqrt{\tau_{i}\tau_{j}d_{i}d_{j}},d_{i}d_{j})}{n-\min(k_{i},k_{j})}

(162)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\min(\mu_{i},\mu_{j})}\max(\tau_{i}d_{i},\tau_{j}d_{j},d_{i}^{2},d_{j}^{2})}{n-\min(k_{i},k_{j})}

(163)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\ell=i,j}\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu_{\ell}}\max(\tau_{\ell}d_{\ell},d_{\ell}^{2})}{n-k_{\ell}}

(164)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq\ell\leq M}\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu_{\ell}}\max(\tau_{\ell}d_{\ell},d_{\ell}^{2})}{n-k_{\ell}}\leq C,

(165)

where the last equality is due to (
138
), and therefore, by Lemma
7
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j})|}\right]=O(1).

(166)

Thus, we proved that under (
138
) each term in the product on the r.h.s. of (
158
) is bounded, and since
M
is finite, the entire product of (
158
) is bounded as well. Finally, to deal with the case where
M=M_{n}
depends on
n
, we note that the proofs of Theorem
6
and Lemma
7
provide uniform bounds on the second moment. To be precise, it is easily seen from the proofs that there is a sufficiently small constant
C>0
such that if the conditions in (
139
) and (
71
) are satisfied, then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime})|}\right]\leq 2,

(167)

and accordingly, the geometric average in (
158
) is bounded by
2
as well. This concludes the proof.

∎

5.3.3
Degree-vertex cover balanced decomposition

In this subsection, we construct a locally
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced decomposition of
\Gamma
, and thus establishing our second argument. We have the following result.

Proposition 3
.

Any non-empty graph
\Gamma
can be decomposed into a set of edge-disjoint subgraphs
\{\Gamma_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{M}
, such that,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq\ell\leq M}\left(\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})\right)\leq 2|e(\Gamma)|\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{1}{M}},

(168)

where the decomposition may includes some empty graphs.

Proof of Proposition
3
.

Let
k\triangleq|v(\Gamma)|
, and fix
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the following recursive decomposition of
\Gamma
to at most
M
edge-disjoint subgraphs
\{\Gamma_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{M}
. Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
be an enumeration of
v(\Gamma)
ordered in a descending ordering according to the graph degrees in
\Gamma
. Let
\ell_{i}\in[|v(\Gamma)|]
denote the maximal index for which
\deg(v_{\ell_{i}})\geq[d_{\max}(\Gamma)]^{\frac{M-i}{M}}
, that is,

\displaystyle\ell_{i}\triangleq\sup\left\{\ell\in[k]:\deg_{\Gamma}(v_{\ell})\geq d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{M-i}{M}}\right\}.

(169)

and set
\ell_{0}\triangleq 0
. We now define
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{M}
recursively. For
\ell=1
, let
E_{1}
be the set of edges with (at least) one of their endpoints is in the set
S_{1}\triangleq\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell_{1}}\}
and
V_{1}
be the set of all vertices in
v(\Gamma)
which participate in an edge of
E_{1}
. We define
\Gamma_{1}
as
\Gamma_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1})
. Given
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{i-1}
, we define
\Gamma_{i}
. We let
E_{i}
be the set of all edges in
e(\Gamma)\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{i-1}E_{j}
with (at least) one of their endpoints is in the set
S_{i}\triangleq\{v_{\ell_{i-1}+1},\ldots,v_{\ell_{i}}\}
, and
V_{i}
be the set of all vertices in
v(\Gamma)
which are one of the endpoints of any edge in
E_{i}
.

Now, we note that for all
i\in[M]
, by the definition of
\Gamma_{i}
, the set
S_{i}
is a vertex cover of
\Gamma_{i}
(perhaps, not a minimal one). Furthermore, we observe that,

\displaystyle d_{\max}(\Gamma_{i})\leq d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{M-i+1}{M}}.

(170)

Indeed, let
v\in V_{i}
be a vertex. If
v\in S_{i}
, then by the definition of
S_{i}
,

\displaystyle\deg_{\Gamma_{i}}(v)\leq\deg_{\Gamma}(v)\leq d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{M-i+1}{M}}.

(171)

In the other case, if
v\in V_{i}\setminus S_{i}
, then assume to the contrary that,

\displaystyle\deg_{\Gamma_{i}}(v)>d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{M-i+1}{M}}.

(172)

By the definition of
S_{1},\dots,S_{i}
, we have that
v\in S_{j}
for some
j<i
. In particular, for any edge
e
which includes
v
we have
e\in E_{j}
, and therefore,

\displaystyle e\notin\left(e(\Gamma)\setminus\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{i-1}E_{\ell}\right)\supseteq E_{i}.

(173)

In particular
v\notin V_{i}
, which is a contradiction. Next, since the smallest degree (in
\Gamma
) of any vertex in
S_{i}
is at least
d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{M-i}{M}}
, we have,

\displaystyle|S_{i}|\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{M-i}{M}}\leq\sum_{\ell=\ell_{i-1}+1}^{\ell_{i}}\deg_{\Gamma}(v_{\ell})\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\deg_{\Gamma}(v_{\ell})\leq 2|e(\Gamma)|,

(174)

and thus,

\displaystyle\tau(\Gamma_{i})

\displaystyle\leq|S_{i}|\leq 2|e(\Gamma)|\cdot d_{\max}^{-\frac{M-i}{M}}(\Gamma).

(175)

Combining the above we get,

\displaystyle\tau(\Gamma_{i})\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma_{i})\leq 2|e(\Gamma)|\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{M-i+1}{M}-\frac{M-i}{M}}=2|e(\Gamma)|\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{1}{M}},

(176)

for all
i\in[M]
. It should be emphasized that it could be the case that some of the subgraphs in the decomposition above might be empty; indeed it could be that
\ell_{i}=\ell_{i+1}
, for some
i\in[M]
, and then
S_{i}
is empty. Nonetheless, the above decomposition is certainly not empty because
\Gamma_{1}\neq\emptyset
. This concludes the proof.
∎

5.3.4
Combining everything together

In this subsection, we combine all the results established in the previous subsections and prove Theorem
7
.

Proof of Theorem
7
.

Let
C>0
be the universal constant from Proposition
2
, and let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of graphs. We separate our proof into two cases. We first consider graphs
\Gamma
with super-logarithmic density, and then those with sub-logarithmic density.

Super-logarithmic density.

Assume that
\mu(\Gamma)\geq\alpha\log|v(\Gamma)|
for a fixed
\alpha>0
. Using Theorem
6
, we know that strong detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\mu(\Gamma)\log(1+\lambda^{2})+\log\left(\max\left(\tau(\Gamma)d_{\max}(\Gamma),d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)\right)\leq\log C+\log n+\log\delta,

(177)

In the super-logarithmic density regime we have,

\displaystyle\log\left(\max\left(\tau(\Gamma),d_{\max}(\Gamma)\right)\right)\leq\log|v(\Gamma)|\leq\frac{\mu(\Gamma)}{\alpha},

(178)

and therefore,

\displaystyle\mu(\Gamma)\log(1+\lambda^{2})+\log\left(\max\left(\tau(\Gamma)d_{\max}(\Gamma),d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)\right)\leq\frac{\alpha\log(1+\lambda^{2})+2}{\alpha}\cdot\mu(\Gamma).

(179)

Thus, (
177
) holds if,

\displaystyle\mu(\Gamma)\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)\alpha}{2+\alpha\log(1+\lambda^{2})}\log n,

(180)

for some
\varepsilon>0
, which proves (
129
). The corresponding upper bound in (
130
) follows from Theorem
5
immediately.

Sub-logarithmic density.

Assume now that
\Gamma
has a sub-logarithmic density, i.e.,
\mu(\Gamma)=o(\log|v(\Gamma)|)
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. From Proposition
3
we know that there exists a decomposition
\Gamma=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{M}\Gamma_{\ell}
, for which (
168
) holds, and we take,

\displaystyle M=\left\lceil\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil.

(181)

By Proposition
2
strong detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\max_{\ell=1,\dots,M}\left(\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu(\Gamma_{\ell})}}{n-|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell}),d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})^{2}\right)\right)\leq C,

(182)

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{M}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}}-1=(1+\lambda^{2})^{4\varepsilon^{-2}}-1.

(183)

Now, by (
168
) and the definition of
\mu(\Gamma)
, we have,

\displaystyle\max_{\ell=1,\dots,M}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu(\Gamma_{\ell})}}{n-|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell}),d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})^{2}\right)\right)

(184)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu(\Gamma)}}{n-|v(\Gamma)|}\cdot\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{1}{M_{n}}},d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)

(185)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\mu(\Gamma)}}{\delta n}\cdot\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{1}{M_{n}}},d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)

(186)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{4\frac{\mu(\Gamma)}{\varepsilon^{2}}}}{\delta n^{1-\frac{1}{M_{n}}}}\cdot\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|,d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)

(187)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{\frac{4\log(1+\lambda^{2})\mu(\Gamma)}{\varepsilon^{2}\log|v(\Gamma)|}}}{\delta n^{1-\frac{1}{M_{n}}}}\cdot\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|,d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)

(188)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{n^{\frac{4\log(1+\lambda^{2})\mu(\Gamma)}{\varepsilon^{2}\log|v(\Gamma)|}}}{\delta n^{1-\frac{1}{M_{n}}}}\cdot\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|,d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right),

(189)

where the first equality follows from the fact that
\mu(\Gamma_{\ell})\leq\mu(\Gamma)
and
|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|\leq|v(\Gamma)|
, for all
\ell\in[M]
, the second equality is because
|v(\Gamma)|\leq(1-\delta)n
, for some fixed
\delta>0
, the third equality follows from
d_{\max}(\Gamma)\leq|v(\Gamma)|
, and the last equality is because
|v(\Gamma)|\leq n
. Since
\mu(\Gamma)=o(\log|v(\Gamma)|)
, we have that for a sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\frac{4\log(1+\lambda^{2})\mu(\Gamma)}{\varepsilon^{2}\log|v(\Gamma)|}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

(190)

and therefore, (
189
) is upper bounded by,

\displaystyle\frac{\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|,d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)}{n^{1-\varepsilon}}.

(191)

Thus, if

\displaystyle\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|,d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)\leq n^{1-\varepsilon},

(192)

then (
182
) is satisfied for a sufficiently large
n
(regardless of the constant
C
), which proves (
131
). Finally, the corresponding upper bound in (
132
) follows from Theorem
5
, for, e.g.,
f(n)=\log_{n}(17\log n)=o(1)
in (
132
).

∎

5.4
Other regimes

In this subsection, we will present and prove the most refined version of our lower bounds, and subsequently analyze those bounds
in the two distinct regimes that garnered the most attention in the literature:

1.

Sparse regime
, where
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(n^{-\alpha})
, for
0<\alpha\leq 2
.

2.

Critical regime
, where
p=1-o(1)
and
q=\Theta(n^{-\alpha})
, for
0\leq\alpha\leq 2
(in particular,
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(n^{\alpha})
).

5.4.1
General bounds

Building on the ideas and concepts introduced in Section
5.3
, in this subsection we prove the following result, which can be viewed as an extension of Theorem
6
.

Theorem 8
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any sequence of graphs
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}]=O(1)
, if at least one of the following hold.

1.

If,

\displaystyle\frac{\left(1+\lambda^{2}\right)^{\mu(\Gamma)}\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)}{n-|v(\Gamma)|}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma),d_{\max}(\Gamma)\right)\leq C.

(193)

2.

If
\mu(\Gamma)\geq 1
and,

\displaystyle\frac{\left(1+\lambda^{2}\right)^{\mu(\Gamma)-1}\cdot\lambda^{2}\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)}{n-|v(\Gamma)|}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}},d_{\max}(\Gamma)\cdot\mu(\Gamma)\right)\leq C.

(194)

3.

If
\mu(\Gamma)<1
and,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)}{n-|v(\Gamma)|}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma),d_{\max}(\Gamma)\cdot\lambda\right)\leq C.

(195)

4.

If
\mu(\Gamma)<1
and,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau(\Gamma)^{2}}{\lambda^{2}\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}}<1-\delta,\quad\text{and }\quad\frac{e\cdot\lambda^{2}\cdot d_{\max}^{2}}{n-|v(\Gamma)|}\geq 1+\delta,

(196)

for some fixed
\delta>0
and,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau(\Gamma)^{2}\cdot\left(e\lambda^{2}d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{2}\right)^{|v(\Gamma)|-1}}{(n-|v(\Gamma)|)^{|v(\Gamma)|}}\leq C.

(197)

5.

If
\limsup\mu(\Gamma)=1-\delta<1
, for some
0<\delta<1
, and,

\displaystyle\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\cdot\max\left(1,\frac{\delta^{2}(n-|v(\Gamma)|)}{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}},\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\frac{e\lambda^{2}}{\delta\left(n-|v(\Gamma)|\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)\leq C.

(198)

The proof of the above theorem follows the same approach to the proof of Theorem
6
. The key difference is that we use an extended version of Lemma
, and bound
|{\cal S}_{m,i,j}|
in multiple different ways (depending on the value of
\mu(\Gamma)
), which surprisingly, are all essential. For simplicity of presentation, we define the following functions,

\displaystyle\kappa(m,\ell,\tau,d)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\tau^{m}(e\tilde{d})^{\ell-m},

(199)

\displaystyle\theta(m,\ell,j,\mu)

\displaystyle\triangleq e^{m}(2\mu)^{\ell-2m}\binom{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor-\ell+m}{j-\ell+m},

(200)

for
2\leq\ell\leq k
,
1\leq m\leq\left\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rfloor
and
\ell-m\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor
, and we let
\tilde{d}=\max(2,d-1)
.

Lemma 8
.

Fix
2\leq\ell\leq k
and
1\leq m\leq\left\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rfloor
.

•

If
\mu\geq 1
, then for
\ell-m\leq j\leq\left\lfloor\mu\cdot\ell\right\rfloor
,

\displaystyle|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa(m,\ell,\tau,d)\cdot\min\left(\binom{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor}{j},\theta(m,\ell,j,\mu)\right),

(201)

and for
\ell-m\leq j\leq\binom{\ell}{2}
,

\displaystyle|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa(m,\ell,\tau,d)\cdot\min\left(\binom{\binom{\ell}{2}}{j},\theta(m,\ell,j,\mu)\right).

(202)

•

If
\mu<1
, then,

\displaystyle|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\kappa(m,\ell,\tau,d)&j=\ell-m\\
0&j\neq\ell-m.\end{cases}

(203)

We will also need a slightly modified version of Lemma
, specifically for the case where
\limsup\mu(\Gamma)<1
.

Lemma 9
.

Assume that
\Gamma
has exactly
L(\Gamma)=L
connected components, each of size at most
t
, and let us assume that that
\mu<1
.
For
1\leq i\leq L
,
2i\leq\ell\leq\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor
,
i\leq m\leq\left\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rfloor
and
j=\ell-m
, let
{\cal S}_{i,m,\ell,j}
be the number of subgraphs of
\Gamma
with exactly
m
connected components,
\ell
vertices,
j
edges, which intersects exactly
i
out of the
L
connected components of
\Gamma
. Then,

\displaystyle|{\cal S}_{i,m,\ell,j}|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\binom{L}{i}t^{i}\binom{i(t-1)}{m-i}m!(e\tilde{d})^{\ell-m}.

(204)

In order to prove the above results we will need some combinatorial results concerning spanning trees.

Definition 9
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a connected undirected graph. A subset of edges
T\subseteq E
is called a spanning tree of
G
if any vertex in
T
is connected to an edge in
T
, and
T=|V|-1
.

The following is a corollary of
[
GM88
, Theorem 1]
.

Proof of
(
201
)
–
(
202
)
.
Let us now bound the number of subgraphs of
G_{U}
. Recall that at this point, we have fixed the vertex set
U
, which contains exactly
p(i)
connected subsets of size
i
. Our goal is to bound the number of ways to choose
j
edges on these graphs while respecting the connectivity constraints. Since every connected component must contain a spanning tree, we can bound the number of ways to choose these edges by multiplying the number of spanning trees of the connected components by the number of ways to choose the remaining edges. Using Lemma
10
, the number of spanning trees
\mathcal{T}(G_{U})
of an induced subgraph
G_{U}
, supported on a set
U
with
i
vertices, is bounded as,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(G_{U})\leq e\left(2\mu(G_{U})\right)^{i-2}\leq e\left(2\mu\right)^{i-2},

(205)

where the second inequality follows from the definition of
\mu(\Gamma)
. Multiplying over all connected components we have at most,

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(e\left(2\mu\right)^{i-2}\right)^{p(i)}=e^{m}(2\mu)^{\ell-2m}

(206)

ways to choose
m
spanning trees, one for each component. Since each spanning tree of a component contains
|v(\mathsf{C}_{i})|-1
edges exactly, there are at most,

\displaystyle|e(G_{U})|-\sum_{i=1}^{m}(|v(\mathsf{C}_{i})|-1)=|e(G_{U})|-\ell+m\leq\min\left(\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor,\binom{\ell}{2}\right)-\ell+m

(207)

edges in the induced subgraph, from which we are to choose the remaining
j-(\ell-m)
edges. Thus, the number of subgraphs for a fixed set of vertices
U
can be upper bounded by,

\displaystyle e^{m}(2\mu)^{\ell-2m}\binom{\min\left(\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor,\binom{\ell}{2}\right)-\ell+m}{j-\ell+m}\triangleq f(\ell,m,j).

(208)

Summing over all possible partitions we obtain,

\displaystyle{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)}\left|S(p,j)\right|

(209)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p\in\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)}\tau^{m}\cdot(e\tilde{d})^{\ell-m}\cdot f(\ell,m,j).

(210)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\tau^{m}\cdot(e\tilde{d})^{\ell-m}\cdot f(\ell,m,j),

(211)

which completes the proof.

Proof of
(
203
)
.
When
\mu<1
the connected components must be all trees. Indeed, since
\mu<1
, for any connected component
{\mathsf{C}}_{i}
we have,

\displaystyle\left|e(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|\leq\mu(\Gamma)\cdot\left|v(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|<1\cdot\left|v(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|.

(212)

Because
\left|v(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|
and
\left|e(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|
are integers, we have
\left|e(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|\leq\left|v(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|-1
. In the other direction, since
\mathsf{C}_{i}
is connected we also have
\left|e(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|\geq\left|v(\mathsf{C}_{i})\right|-1
, and thus equality holds. Hence, in this case, the number of edges of a subgraph
\mathsf{H}
with exactly
m
connected components is
\ell-m
. The proof then follows by repeating the exact same steps as in the case where
\mu\geq 1
, with the only observation that now a choice of the
m
connected components determine a unique subgraph in
{\cal S}_{\ell,m,j}
, for any
j
such that
{\cal S}_{\ell,m,j}\neq\emptyset
.

∎

Proof of
(
194
)
.
From (
201
) we know that,

\displaystyle|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|\leq\kappa(m,\ell,\tau,d)\cdot\theta(m,\ell,j,\mu).

(213)

Then, applying Lemmas
4
and (
213
) on (
5.3.1
), we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L(G)}^{2}]

(214)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor}|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|\lambda^{2j}\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(215)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor}\lambda^{2j}\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\tau^{m}(ed)^{\ell-m}e^{m}(2\mu)^{\ell-2m}\binom{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor-\ell+m}{j-\ell+m}

(216)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{2e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor}\lambda^{2(j-\ell+m)}\binom{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor-\ell+m}{j-\ell+m}

(217)

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{2e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor-\ell+m}\lambda^{2j^{\prime}}\binom{\left\lfloor\mu\ell\right\rfloor-\ell+m}{j^{\prime}}

(218)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{2e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu^{2}}\right)^{m}\cdot(1+\lambda^{2})^{\mu\ell-\ell+m}

(219)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{2e(1+\varepsilon)(1+\lambda^{2})^{\mu-1}\cdot\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\cdot\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})\tau^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu^{2}}\right)^{m}.

(220)

We now separate our analysis into two complementary cases. In the first, we assume that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})\tau^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu^{2}}\geq 1+\alpha,

(221)

for some fixed positive
\alpha
. In this case, the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
220
) is dominated by
\left(\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})\tau^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu^{2}}\right)^{\ell/2}
, and therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}\right]\leq 1+C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)(1+\lambda^{2})^{\mu-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\tau d}{\sqrt{2}(n-k)}\right)^{\ell},

(222)

which is bounded (and accordingly strong detection is impossible) provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{\mu-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\tau d}{n-k}<C,

(223)

for some universal
C>0
. Next, we move to the other case, where,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})\tau^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu^{2}}\leq 1+o(1),

(224)

for some
o(1)
function. Here, for a sufficiently large
n
, the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
220
) is dominated by
(1+\varepsilon)^{\ell}
, and therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L(G)}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{2e(1+\varepsilon)^{2}(1+\lambda^{2})^{\mu-1}\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}.

(225)

The above is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{\mu-1}\lambda^{2}d^{2}\mu}{n-k}<C,

(226)

for some universal constant
C>0
. Since (
221
) holds exactly when (
223
) dominates (
226
) (perhaps up to a multiplicative constant factor), we have that (
194
) is sufficient for
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}]
to be bounded.

Proof of
(
195
)
-
(
197
)
.
We are now in the regime where
\mu<1
. We repeat the analysis as in the pervious case, but bound
|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|
using (
203
). Specifically, in this case, we sum over the vertices
\ell
and connected components
m
of
\mathsf{H}
, while the number of edges
j
is fixed and given by
j=\ell-m
, as explained in the proof of (
203
). Specifically, we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L(G)}^{2}]

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|\cdot\lambda^{2(\ell-m)}\cdot\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(227)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}\lambda^{2(\ell-m)}\cdot\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\tau^{m}(ed)^{\ell-m}

(228)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}d^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}\right)^{m}.

(229)

Again, we separate our analysis into two complementary cases. In the first, we assume that,

\displaystyle\frac{2\tau^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}\geq 1+\alpha,

(230)

for some fixed positive
\alpha
. In this case, the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
220
) is dominated by
\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}\right)^{\ell/2}
, and therefore, we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}\right]\leq 1+C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2e}(1+\varepsilon)\lambda\tau d}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}.

(231)

The above is bounded whenever

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda\tau d}{n-k}<C,

(232)

for some constant
C>0
. Next, we move to the other case, where,

\displaystyle\frac{2\tau^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}\leq 1+o(1),

(233)

for some
o(1)
function. Here, for a sufficiently large
n
, the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
220
) is dominated by
\left(1+\varepsilon\right)^{\ell}
, and thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L(G)}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)^{2}\lambda^{2}d^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}<C.

(234)

The above is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}d^{2}}{n-k}<C,

(235)

for some constant
C
. Since (
230
) holds exactly when (
232
) dominates (
234
) (perhaps, up to a multiplicative constant factor), we have that (
195
) is sufficient for
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}]
to be bounded.

In order to prove (
196
), consider the scenario where

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}\leq 1-\alpha,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}{n-k}>1+\alpha,

(236)

for some fixed constant
\alpha
. In this case, the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
229
) is dominated by
\frac{\tau^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}
, while the outer sum is dominated by
\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}d^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{k}
. We therefore have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L(G)}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}\cdot d^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{k},

(237)

which is bounded if,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}\cdot(e\lambda^{2}d^{2})^{k-1}}{(n-k)^{k}}=O(1).

(238)

This proves that (
196
) and (
197
) are sufficient for the impossibility of strong detection.

Proof of
(
198
)
.
Consider the case where,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu=1-\delta,

(239)

for
\delta>0
. Here, as explained before,
\Gamma
must be a forest. We begin with the observation that in this case each tree in
\Gamma
contains at most
\frac{1}{\delta}
vertices. Indeed, if
\mathsf{T}
is a tree in
\Gamma
with
\ell
vertices, we have

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{\ell}=\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}=\mu(\mathsf{T})\leq\mu\leq 1-\delta.

(240)

Let us denote
t=\frac{1}{\delta}
, which as stated above, is a constant that bounds the sizes of the connected components in
\Gamma
, and in particular, the maximum degree of
\Gamma
. Recall that
L=L(\Gamma)
denotes the number of connected components of
\Gamma
. We note that,

\displaystyle\frac{k}{t}\leq L\leq\frac{k}{2}.

(241)

We also observe that any vertex cover set must contain at least one vertex from each connected component of
\Gamma
, and therefore, we have
L\leq\tau
. On the other hand, from the definition of
\tau
we have
\tau\leq k
. Combining together, we get,

\displaystyle\frac{k}{t}\leq L\leq\tau\leq k\leq t\cdot L,

(242)

where the first inequity holds for a sufficiently large
n
, as
t
is bounded with
n
.

We next evaluate (
96
) by running over subgraphs with fixed number of vertices and connected components, but also take into count the number
i(\mathsf{H})
of connected components of
\Gamma
which intersects with
\mathsf{H}
. Formally, let
{\cal S}_{i,m,\ell,j}
be the number of subgraphs
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma
with
m
connected components,
\ell
vertices, and
j
edges, which satisfy
i(\mathsf{H})=i
. As in the previous case, if
j\neq\ell-m
, we have
S_{i,m,\ell,j}=0
. We note that with
i
fixed, the number of vertices
\ell
must be between
2i
and
it
(which bounds the maximal number of vertices in the
i
connected components of
\Gamma)
. With
i
and
\ell
fixed, the number of connected components
m
must be between
i
and
\left\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rfloor
. Combining the above observations with (
96
), Lemmas
4
and
9
we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L(G)}^{2}]

(243)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}|{\cal S}_{i,m,\ell,\ell-m}|\lambda^{2(\ell-m)}\cdot\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(244)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}|{\cal S}_{i,m,\ell,\ell-m}|\lambda^{2(\ell-m)}\cdot\frac{(2tL)^{m}t^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(245)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\binom{L}{i}t^{i}\binom{i(t-1)}{m-i}m!(ed)^{\ell-m}\lambda^{2(\ell-m)}\cdot\frac{(2tL)^{m}t^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(246)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\binom{L}{i}t^{i}\binom{i(t-1)}{m-i}m!(et)^{\ell-m}\lambda^{2(\ell-m)}\cdot\frac{(2tL)^{m}t^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(247)

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{L}t^{i}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\frac{L!}{(L-i)!}\cdot\frac{(i(t-1))!}{\left(i(t-1)-(m-i)\right)!}\binom{m}{i}\left(\frac{2L}{et\lambda^{2}}\right)^{m}

(248)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{L}t^{i}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\frac{L!}{(L-i)!}\cdot\frac{(L(t-1))!}{\left(L(t-1)-(m-i)\right)!}\left(\frac{em}{i}\right)^{i}\left(\frac{2L}{et\lambda^{2}}\right)^{m}

(249)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{L}t^{i}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}L^{i}(L(t-1))^{m-i}\left(\frac{et}{2}\right)^{i}\left(\frac{2L}{et\lambda^{2}}\right)^{m}

(250)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{L}t^{i}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}L^{i}(Lt)^{m-i}\left(\frac{et}{2}\right)^{i}\left(\frac{2L}{et\lambda^{2}}\right)^{m}

(251)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\left(\frac{et}{2}\right)^{i}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2L^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}}\right)^{m}

(252)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{et}{2}\right)^{i}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=i}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2k^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}}\right)^{m},

(253)

where (a) follows from the fact that
\binom{n}{m}\leq\left(\frac{en}{m}\right)^{m}
, for any
m\leq n
, and (b) is because
m\leq\frac{1}{2}\ell\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot t\cdot i
. We separate our analysis into two complementary cases. In the first, we assume that,

\displaystyle\frac{2k^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}}\leq 1-\alpha,

(254)

and

\displaystyle\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\geq 1+\alpha,

(255)

for a fixed
\alpha>0
. Then, we bound the above sum as,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L(G)}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{k^{2}t}{\lambda^{2}}\right)^{i}\sum_{\ell=2i}^{\left\lfloor it\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}.

(256)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{k^{2}t\left(e(1+\varepsilon)t\lambda^{2}\right)^{t}}{\lambda^{2}(n-k)^{t}}\right)^{i},

(257)

which is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}t^{t+1}\lambda^{2(t-1)}}{(n-k)^{t}}\leq C,

(258)

which concludes the proof of (
198
). We remark that we do not need to analyze the regimes where

\displaystyle\frac{2k^{2}}{e\lambda^{2}}>1+\alpha,

(259)

or,

\displaystyle\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{n-k}\leq 1-\alpha,

(260)

because the resulting conditions overlap with (
195
)–(
197
).

∎

5.4.2

Reduction to the vertex cover-degree balanced case

In this subsection, we extend the decomposition argument in Proposition
2
in the dense regime, to the more general scenario we consider here, using the refined combinatorial analysis developed in the proof of Theorem
8
.

Proposition 4
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of graphs, and assume that each can be decomposed into a set of edge-disjoint graphs, i.e.,
\Gamma=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{M}\Gamma_{\ell}
. Then,
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}]=O(1)
if

\displaystyle\max_{\ell=1,\dots,M}\left(\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\tilde{\mu}(\Gamma_{\ell})-1}\lambda_{M}^{2}}{n-|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})\sqrt{\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})}{\lambda_{M}^{2}}},d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})^{2}\mu(\Gamma_{\ell})\right)\right)\leq C,

(261)

for some universal
C>0
, where
\lambda_{M}^{2}\triangleq(1+\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}}-1
and
\tilde{\mu}(\Gamma_{\ell})=\max(\mu(\Gamma_{\ell}),1)
.

Proof.

The proof follows the exact same steps as in the proof of Proposition
2
. Specifically, applying Hölder inequality on (
97
) we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|}\right]\leq\prod_{i,j=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{i}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2}_{M})^{|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime})|}\right]^{\frac{1}{M^{2}}}.

(262)

Accordingly, in order to find the conditions under which (
262
) is bounded it remains to generalize the argument of Lemma
7
, for our case. Let us follow the notation used in the proof of Lemma
7
, where
\tau_{i}=\tau(\Gamma_{i})
,
d_{i}=d_{\max}(\Gamma_{i})
,
\mu_{i}=\mu(\Gamma_{i})
,
k_{i}=|v(\Gamma_{i})|
,
\bar{k}=\min(k_{1},k_{2})
, and
\bar{\mu}=\min(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. Below we prove that
\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]
is bounded if the following hold:

1.

If
\bar{\mu}<1
, and

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{n-\bar{k}}\cdot\max\left(\sqrt{\tau_{1}d_{1}\tau_{2}d_{2}},d_{1}d_{2}\lambda\right)\leq C,

(263)

for some universal
C>0
.

2.

If
\bar{\mu}\geq 1
, and

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}-1}\lambda^{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\cdot\max\left(\sqrt{\frac{\tau_{1}d_{1}\tau_{2}d_{2}(1+\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{2}}},d_{1}d_{2}\bar{\mu}\right)\leq C,

(264)

for some universal
C>0
.

We start with the case where
\bar{\mu}>1
. By symmetry, the distributions of
|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|
and
|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}^{\prime})|
are exactly the same, where
\Gamma_{i}
is a uniform random copy and
\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}
is a fixed copy in
{\cal K}_{n}
. Thus, we can assume, without loss of generality, that
\bar{\mu}=\mu_{1}
. As in the proof of Lemma
7
, we apply Lemma
4
and the bound in (
201
) on (
102
), and get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]

(265)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}]

(266)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\left\lfloor\ell\bar{\mu}\right\rfloor}|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}(\Gamma_{1})|\lambda^{2j}\frac{(2\tau_{2})^{m}d_{2}^{\ell-m}}{(n-{\bar{k}})^{\ell}}

(267)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\frac{(2\tau_{2})^{m}d_{2}^{\ell-m}}{(n-{\bar{k}})^{\ell}}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\left\lfloor\ell\bar{\mu}\right\rfloor}\lambda^{2j}\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\tau_{1}^{m}(ed_{1})^{\ell-m}e^{m}(2\mu_{1})^{\ell-2m}\binom{\left\lfloor\ell\bar{\mu}\right\rfloor-\ell+m}{j-\ell+m}

(268)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{2e(1+\varepsilon)d_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}\bar{\mu}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{2d_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}\bar{\mu}^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\ell\bar{\mu}\right\rfloor-\ell+m}\lambda^{2j^{\prime}}\binom{\left\lfloor\ell\bar{\mu}\right\rfloor-\ell+m}{j^{\prime}}

(269)

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{2e(1+\varepsilon)(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}-1}d_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}\bar{\mu}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}(1+\lambda^{2})}{2d_{1}d_{1}\lambda^{2}\bar{\mu}^{2}}\right)^{m}.

(270)

We separate our analysis into two complementary cases. In the first, we assume that,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}(1+\lambda^{2})}{2d_{1}d_{1}\lambda^{2}\bar{\mu}^{2}}>1+\alpha,

(271)

for some fixed positive
\alpha
. Then, we bound the above sum as,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\sqrt{2d_{1}d_{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\right)^{\ell},

(272)

which is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\sqrt{d_{1}d_{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}{n-{\bar{k}}}<C,

(273)

for some universal
C>0
. Next, we move to the other case, where,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}(1+\lambda^{2})}{2d_{1}d_{1}\lambda^{2}\bar{\mu}^{2}}\leq 1+o(1),

(274)

in which case the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
270
) is dominated by
(1+\varepsilon)^{\ell}
, and therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{2e(1+\varepsilon)^{2}(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}-1}d_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}\bar{\mu}}{n-{\bar{k}}}\right)^{\ell},

(275)

which is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda^{2})^{\bar{\mu}-1}\lambda^{2}d_{1}d_{2}\bar{\mu}}{n-{\bar{k}}}<C,

(276)

for some universal
C>0
. Since (
271
) holds exactly when (
273
) dominates (
276
), we get that
\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]=O(1)
if (
264
) holds.

Next, we proceed to the case where
\mu_{1}=\bar{\mu}<1
. This case follows similarly to the previous one, with the only difference being the use of the bound in (
203
). Specifically, we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,\ell-m}(\Gamma_{1})|\lambda^{2(\ell-m)}\frac{(2\tau_{2})^{m}d_{2}^{\ell-m}}{(n-{\bar{k}})^{\ell}}

(277)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)|\tau_{1}^{m}(ed_{1})^{\ell-m}\lambda^{2(\ell-m)}\frac{(2\tau_{2})^{m}d_{2}^{\ell-m}}{(n-{\bar{k}})^{\ell}}

(278)

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\bar{k}}\left(\frac{e(1+\varepsilon)d_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}}\right)^{m}.

(279)

Similarly to the previous case, if the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
279
) is dominated by the last term then (
279
) is bounded if,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda\sqrt{d_{1}d_{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}{n-k}<C,

(280)

for some universal
C>0
. Otherwise, if the common ratio of the inner sum is bounded by
1+o(1)
, then (
279
) is bounded if,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}d_{1}d_{2}}{n-k}<C,

(281)

for some universal
C>0
. Since the inner sum is dominated by the last term exactly when the condition in (
280
) dominates (
281
), we get that
\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}\right]=O(1)
if (
263
) holds. Finally, note that for any
i,j\in[M]
, the terms on the l.h.s. of (
263
) and (
264
) are bounded by the term on the l.h.s. of (
261
). This implies that (
261
), with an appropriate choice of
C>0
, is a sufficient condition for

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{i}}\left[(1+\lambda^{2}_{M})^{|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime})|}\right]^{\frac{1}{M^{2}}}\leq 2,

(282)

and therefore the entire product in (
262
) is bounded as well, which concludes the proof.
∎

5.4.3

The sparse regime

In this subsection, we specialize the general bounds from Theorem
8
to the sparse regime, where
\chi^{2}(p||q)=(n^{-\alpha})
, for
0<\alpha\leq 2
. This occurs when
|p-q|,p,q=\Theta(n^{-\alpha})
. In this regime, we focus on the polynomial (or more precisely, signomial, as we allow for non-integer exponents) growth/decay of each of the parameters, rather than the sub-polynomial factors, although our bounds apply also to those. Accordingly, we assume that
k\triangleq|v(\Gamma_{n})|=\Theta(n^{\beta})
, for
0<\beta\leq 1
, and investigate the statistical limits of our detection problem in terms of the exponential relations between the parameters
\mu(\Gamma),|e(\Gamma)|,|v(\Gamma)|
and
d_{\max}(\Gamma)
. Consider the following definition.

Definition 10
.

A sequence of graphs
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
is called an
(\epsilon,\delta,\zeta)
-polynomial family if,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log|e(\Gamma)|}{\log|v(\Gamma)|}=\epsilon,\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log d_{\max}(\Gamma)}{\log|v(\Gamma)|}=\delta,\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\mu(\Gamma)}{\log|v(\Gamma)|}=\zeta.

(283)

The following is our main result for the sparse regime.

Theorem 9
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be an
(\epsilon,\delta,\zeta)
-polynomial family of graphs with
k=|v(\Gamma)|=\Theta(n^{\beta})
,
0<\beta<1
, and let
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(n^{-\alpha})
, for
0<\alpha<2
. Then, weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\beta<\min\left(\frac{\alpha}{\zeta},\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta+\zeta},\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}\right),

(284)

while strong detection is possible if,

\displaystyle\beta>\min\left(\frac{\alpha}{\zeta},\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta},\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}\right),

(285)

where
\frac{\alpha}{a}\triangleq\infty
if
a=0
.

Proof.

The barrier in (
285
) is obtained by projecting Theorem
5
onto the
(\epsilon,\delta,\zeta)
-polynomial family.
Specifically, it is immediately seen that the scan test is successful if
\beta>\frac{\alpha}{\zeta}
. The count test is successful if

\displaystyle\beta>\max\left(\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon},\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}\right).

(286)

Note that in region
\alpha\in[0,2]
we always have
\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}>\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}
meaning that the maximum is dominated by
\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}
. Finally, the condition in (
25
) implies that the maximum degree test succeeds if

\displaystyle\beta>\max\left(\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta},\frac{\alpha}{\delta}\right).

(287)

Since
0<\beta\leq 1
we claim that
\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta}
dominates the maximum in (
287
) in the feasible regime. Indeed, observe that
\frac{\alpha}{\delta}
dominates the maximum exactly when
\alpha>1
. Because
0<\delta\leq 1
, we have
\frac{\alpha}{\delta}>1
, and therefore,
\beta
cannot exceed
\frac{\alpha}{\delta}
.
Therefore, the maximum degree test achieves strong detection provided that
\beta>\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta}
, which concludes the proof of (
285
).

Let us now prove (
284
). For simplicity, we start by assuming that
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced, and then generalize. For
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced graphs,

\displaystyle\tau(\Gamma)\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)=|e(\Gamma)|^{1+o(1)}=k^{\epsilon+o(1)}.

(288)

Then, Theorem
8
implies that weak detection is impossible if (
194
) holds, which translates to,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+n^{-\alpha+o(1)})^{k^{\zeta+o(1)}}n^{-\alpha+o(1)}}{n^{1+o(1)}}\max\left(\frac{k^{\epsilon+o(1)}}{n^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}+o(1)}},k^{2\delta+\zeta+o(1)}\right)\leq C.

(289)

Since
k=\Theta(n^{\beta})
, the above is equivalent to,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+n^{-\alpha+o(1)})^{n^{\zeta\beta}}n^{-\alpha}}{n^{1+o(1)}}\max\left(\frac{n^{\epsilon\beta}}{n^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}},n^{(2\delta+\zeta)\beta}\right)\leq C.

(290)

A simple calculation reveals that the l.h.s. of (
290
) decays to zero if,

\displaystyle\beta<\min\left(\frac{\alpha}{\zeta},\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta+\zeta},\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}\right),

(291)

as stated in (
284
). In order to reduce the general case to the
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced scenario, we repeat the ideas in the proof of Theorem
7
, where we have used the
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced decomposition argument presented in Subsection
5.3.3
. Assume that (
284
) holds, and let us denote,

\displaystyle 2\rho\triangleq\beta-\min\left(\frac{\alpha}{\zeta},\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta+\zeta},\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}\right)>0.

(292)

Using Proposition
3
, consider the decomposition of
\Gamma
into
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{M}
, where
M=\left\lceil\rho^{-1}\right\rceil
, such that for all
1\leq\ell\leq M
, we have

\displaystyle\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})

\displaystyle\leq|e(\Gamma)|d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{\frac{1}{M}}\leq n^{\beta(\epsilon+\rho)+o(1)}.

(293)

Denote
k_{\ell}\triangleq|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|
,
d_{\ell}\triangleq d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})
,
\mu_{\ell}\triangleq\mu(\Gamma_{\ell})
, and
\tau_{\ell}\triangleq\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})
, for all
1\leq\ell\leq M
. Furthermore, recall that
d\triangleq d_{\max}(\Gamma)
,
\mu\triangleq\mu(\Gamma)
, and
\tau\triangleq\tau(\Gamma)
. By
(
293
) observe that for all
\ell\in[M]
,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\max(\mu_{\ell},1)-1}\lambda_{M}^{2}}{n-k_{\ell}}\max\left(\tau_{\ell}d_{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{1+\lambda_{M}^{2}}{\lambda_{M}^{2}}},d_{\ell}^{2}\mu_{\ell}\right)

(294)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\max(\mu,1)-1}\lambda_{M}^{2}}{n-k}\max\left(\tau_{\ell}d_{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{1+\lambda_{M}^{2}}{\lambda_{M}^{2}}},d^{2}\mu\right)

(295)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{n^{\beta\zeta+o(1)}}\lambda_{M}^{2}}{n^{1+o(1)}}\max\left(n^{\epsilon\beta+\rho}\sqrt{\frac{1+\lambda_{M}^{2}}{\lambda_{M}^{2}}},n^{\beta(2\delta+\zeta)}\right).

(296)

Furthermore, note that for a finite
M
independent of
n
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{M}^{2}=(1+\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}}-1=M^{2}\lambda^{2}+O(\lambda^{4})=n^{-\alpha+o(1)}.

(297)

Thus, using (
296
) we get,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\lambda_{M}^{2})^{\max(\mu_{\ell},1)-1}\lambda_{M}^{2}}{n-k_{\ell}}\max\left(\tau_{\ell}d_{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{1+\lambda_{M}^{2}}{\lambda_{M}^{2}}},d_{\ell}^{2}\mu_{\ell}\right)

(298)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{(1+n^{-\alpha+o(1)})^{n^{\beta\zeta+o(1)}}n^{-\alpha}}{n^{1+o(1)}}\max\left(\frac{n^{\epsilon\beta+\rho}}{n^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}},n^{\beta(2\delta+\zeta)}\right).

(299)

Under (
292
), the expression in (
299
) is upper bounded by
n^{-\rho+o(1)}=o(1)
. In particular, the condition in Proposition
4
is satisfied, which imply that weak detection is impossible.
∎

As can seen from Theorem
9
, our bounds are not tight in general. In fact, for any set of parameters
(\zeta,\delta,\epsilon)
for which the set,

\displaystyle A\triangleq\left\{0<\alpha<2~\Big|~\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta+\zeta}>\min\left(\frac{\alpha}{\zeta},\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}\right)\right\},

(300)

is non-empty, the lower and upper bounds in Theorem
9
do not match. Nonetheless, in the following, we show that for several special non-trivial families of graphs our bounds are, in fact, tight.

A condition for tightness.

A simple algebraic analysis shows that in some non-empty region of the possible values of
(\zeta,\delta,\epsilon)
in the parameter space, the set
A
can be empty (and then our bounds are tight). This is captured in the following result.

Lemma 11

(Optimality of the count and scan tests)
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be an
(\epsilon,\delta,\zeta)
-polynomial family of graphs with
\delta,\zeta>0
,
k=|v(\Gamma)|=\Theta(k^{\beta})
, for
0<\beta<1
, and let
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(n^{-\alpha})
, for
0<\alpha<2
. If

\displaystyle\epsilon>2\delta+\frac{\zeta}{2},

(301)

then weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\beta<\min\left(\frac{\alpha}{\zeta},\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}\right),

(302)

while strong detection is possible if,

\displaystyle\beta>\min\left(\frac{\alpha}{\zeta},\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}\right).

(303)

Proof.

For the set
A
in (
300
) is empty, we investigate when
\beta\leq\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}
is dominated by
\beta\leq\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta+\zeta}
. In the parameter space,these two lines cross at,

\displaystyle\alpha=\alpha_{0}\triangleq\frac{2(\epsilon-2\delta-\zeta)}{2\delta+\zeta-2\epsilon}.

(304)

Accordingly, the inequality
\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon}>\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta+\zeta}
is possible only in one of the following two cases:

•

If,

\epsilon<\frac{2\delta+\zeta}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha>\alpha_{0}.

(305)

In this case, for
A
to be empty, the intersection point must satisfy
\alpha_{0}>2
, which holds if and only if,

\displaystyle\epsilon>\frac{2(2\delta+\zeta)}{3}.

(306)

The intersection of the conditions on the l.h.s. of (
305
) and (
306
) is empty.

•

If,

\epsilon>\frac{2\delta+\zeta}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha<\alpha_{0}.

(307)

In this case, for
A
to be empty, the interval
[0,\alpha_{0})
must be contained in the region where the line
\beta=\frac{\alpha}{\zeta}
is below
\beta=\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta+\zeta}
. A straightforward calculation shows that these lines intersect at
\alpha_{1}\triangleq\frac{\zeta}{2\delta}
, and accordingly, the line
\beta=\frac{\alpha}{\zeta}
is below the line
\beta=\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta+\zeta}
, if
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}
. Thus, the set
A
is empty if
\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{0}
which boils down to,

\displaystyle\frac{2(\epsilon-2\delta-\zeta)}{2\delta+\zeta-2\epsilon}<\frac{\zeta}{\delta},

(308)

and is equivalent to,

\displaystyle\epsilon>\frac{6\zeta\delta+\zeta^{2}+8\delta^{2}}{4\delta+2\zeta}=\frac{(2\delta+\zeta)^{2}+4\delta^{2}+2\zeta\delta}{2(2\delta+\zeta)}=\frac{2\delta+\zeta}{2}+\frac{4\delta^{2}+2\zeta\delta}{2(2\delta+\zeta)}=2\delta+\frac{\zeta}{2}.

(309)

We conclude by noting that the condition
\epsilon>2\delta+\frac{\zeta}{2}
dominates the condition
\epsilon>\frac{2\delta+\zeta}{2}
.

∎

The condition in (
301
) is satisfied for several non-trivial families of graphs. As an example consider the family of graphs with moderate degrees, defined as follows: Let
(\epsilon,\delta,\zeta)
be a polynomial sequence of graphs, where
\delta\leq\frac{2}{5}
(namely,
d_{\max}(\Gamma)\ll k^{2/5}
). In this case, the set
A
is empty and our lower and upper bounds align. Indeed, note that by the definition of maximal density, we have
\delta>\zeta
, and since we consider only graphs containing no isolated vertices we also have that,

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle\geq 1\geq\frac{5\delta}{2}\geq 2\delta+\frac{\zeta}{2}.

(310)

Thus, the condition in (
301
) is satisfied.

Sub-polynomial density.

We consider now a family of graphs, for which the condition in Lemma (
301
) is not (necessarily) satisfied, but still Theorem
9
gives tight results. Consider the following definition.

Definition 11
.

A sequence of graphs
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
has sub-polynomial density if,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log(\mu(\Gamma))}{\log|v(\Gamma)|}=0.

(311)

Otherwise,
\Gamma
has super-polynomial density.

For graphs
\Gamma
with sub-polynomial density it must be that
\zeta=0
, and then we see that the lower and upper bounds in Theorem
9
coincide. Specifically, weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\beta<\min\left(\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon},\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta}\right),

(312)

while strong detection is possible if,

\displaystyle\beta>\min\left(\frac{2+\alpha}{2\epsilon},\frac{1+\alpha}{2\delta}\right).

(313)

Note that here the upper bounds are achieved by the count and maximum degree tests, which run in polynomial-time. As so, there is no computational-statistical gap for detecting graphs with sub-polynomial density. In Section
6
, we will prove that for graphs with super-polynomial density, a computational-statistical gap exist always.

Super-dense graphs.

Consider the following family of graphs.

Definition 12
.

A sequence of graphs
\Gamma=(\Gamma)_{n}
is super-dense if,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}=\frac{\log(\mu(\Gamma))}{\log|v(\Gamma)|}=1.

(314)

Examples for super-dense graphs are: cliques, and balanced bipartite graphs (i.e., bipartite graphs with the same order of left and right vertices). We have the following result.

Lemma 12
.

Any family of super-dense graphs is a
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced
(2,1,1)
-polynomial family.

Proof of Lemma
12
.

We begin by observing that from the definition of
\mu(\Gamma)
, we have
\mu(\Gamma)\leq d_{\max}(\Gamma)
. Thus, (
314
) implies that,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log d_{\max}(\Gamma)}{\log|v(\Gamma)|}\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\mu(\Gamma)}{\log|v(\Gamma)|}=1.

(315)

In addition, note that if
\mu(\Gamma)=k^{1+o(1)}
then
|e(\Gamma)|=k^{2+o(1)}
. To see that, let
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be a subgraph of
\Gamma
with maximal density. We have,
