\displaystyle k^{1+o(1)}=\mu(\Gamma)=\frac{|E^{\prime}|}{|V^{\prime}|}\leq\frac{\binom{|V^{\prime}|}{2}}{|V^{\prime}|}\leq\frac{|V^{\prime}|}{2},

(316)

and in particular,

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}}{2}\geq|e(\Gamma)|\geq|E^{\prime}|=\mu(\Gamma)\cdot|V^{\prime}|\geq 2\cdot\mu(\Gamma)^{2}=k^{2+o(1)}.

(317)

Combined with (
315
), we get that
\Gamma
is a
(2,1,1)
-polynomial family. Finally, we note that for any graph
\Gamma
, we have
\mu(\Gamma)\leq\tau(\Gamma)
. To see that, let
S\subseteq V
be a minimal vertex cover of
\Gamma
, and let
\Gamma^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be a subgraph such that
|E^{\prime}|/|V^{\prime}|=\mu(\Gamma)
. Since any edge in
\Gamma
must contain a vertex in
S
, the number of edges in
\Gamma
, which intersect
V^{\prime}
, is at most
|V^{\prime}|\cdot|S|
. In particular, we have
|E^{\prime}|\leq|V^{\prime}|\cdot|S|
, and therefore,

\displaystyle k^{1+o(1)}=\mu(\Gamma)=\frac{|E^{\prime}|}{|V^{\prime}|}\leq\frac{|S|\cdot|V^{\prime}|}{|V^{\prime}|}=|S|=\tau(\Gamma)\leq k.

(318)

This proves that
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced.
∎

Consider the case where
\Gamma_{n}
is a clique with
n^{\beta}
vertices. The bound in Theorem
9
imply that detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\beta<\min\left(\alpha,\frac{\alpha+1}{3}\right).

(319)

This bound is loose. Indeed, as was shown in
[
HWX15a
]
, detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\beta<\min\left(\alpha,\frac{\alpha}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\right),

(320)

while possible if
\beta
is exceeds the above threshold. This sub-optimality is somewhat expected in the light of Lemma
12
. Indeed, the bounding technique used in Theorem
8
hings on the evaluation of the second moment of the likelihood, by running over subgraphs with a fixed number of connected components, and bounding the number of such subgraphs in terms of
\mu(\Gamma)
and
\tau(\Gamma)
. When
\tau(\Gamma)
and
\mu(\Gamma)
are large w.r.t.
|v(\Gamma)|
, this bound becomes loose. Furthermore, our bound in Lemma
4
, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathsf{H}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}\right]\leq\frac{(2\tau(\Gamma))^{m}\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma)^{|v(\mathsf{H})|-m}}{(n-|v(\Gamma)|^{|v(\mathsf{H})|})},

(321)

becomes exponentially equivalent to the trivial bound,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma\right]\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left[v(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})\subseteq v(\Gamma)\right]\leq\left(\frac{|v(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})|}{n-|v(\Gamma)|}\right)^{|v(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})|},

(322)

indicating that bounding the likelihood’s second moment by summing subgraphs according to their connected components does not exploit any special properties of the planted structure, and therefore, results in a sub-optimal lower bound.

As a remedy, we now show that by slightly modifying our techniques—essentially by ignoring the number of connected components—we obtain tight results.

Proposition 5
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})
be a sequence of super-dense graphs, with
|v(\Gamma)|=\Theta\left(n^{\beta}\right)
, and let
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta\left(n^{-\alpha}\right)
, with
0<\alpha\leq 2
and
0\leq\beta\leq 1
. Then, weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\beta<\min\left(\alpha,\frac{\alpha}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\right),

(323)

while strong detection is possible if,

\displaystyle\beta>\min\left(\alpha,\frac{\alpha}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\right).

(324)

Proof.

The barrier in (
324
) follows immediately from Theorem
9
and Lemma
12
. Indeed, since
\Gamma
is a
(2,1,1)
-polynomial family, a simple calculation shows that,

\displaystyle\min\left(\frac{\alpha}{1},\frac{1+\alpha}{2\cdot 1},\frac{2+\alpha}{2\cdot 2}\right)=\min\left(\alpha,\frac{\alpha}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\right).

(325)

We now prove that detection is impossible when (
323
) holds. Assume that
\beta<\alpha
. We repeat the main arguments used in the proof of Theorem
8
, where we sum over subgraphs with a fixed number of edges
j
and vertices
\ell
; we do not constrain/fix the number of connected components. Note that for a fixed
\ell
, the number of subgraphs of
\Gamma
with
\ell
vertices and
\ell/2\leq j\leq\binom{\ell}{2}
edges is at most,

\displaystyle\binom{k}{\ell}\cdot\binom{\binom{\ell}{2}}{j}.

(326)

Thus, by Lemma
4
and (
322
) we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma\right]\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}

(327)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\sum_{j=\left\lceil\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rceil}^{\binom{\ell}{2}}\binom{k}{\ell}\binom{\binom{\ell}{2}}{j}\lambda^{2j}\left(\frac{k}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}

(328)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{ek^{2}}{(n-k)\ell}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{j=\left\lceil\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rceil}^{\binom{\ell}{2}}\left(\frac{e\binom{\ell}{2}}{j}\lambda^{2}\right)^{j}

(329)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{ek^{2}}{(n-k)\ell}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{j=\left\lceil\frac{\ell}{2}\right\rceil}^{\binom{\ell}{2}}\left(e(\ell-1)\lambda^{2}\right)^{j}

(330)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}1+C\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\left(\frac{e^{2}k^{2}\sqrt{\lambda^{2}}}{(n-k)}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\ell-1}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell}

(331)

where
(a)
is because we sum over subgraphs containing no isolated vertices which implies that
j\geq\left\lceil\ell/2\right\rceil
,
(b)
follows from the fact that
\binom{n}{m}\leq\left(\frac{en}{m}\right)^{m}
, for any
m\leq n
, and in
(c)
we used the assumption that
\beta<\alpha
, and so
(\ell-1)\lambda^{2}\leq k\lambda^{2}=o(1)
. The sum in (
331
) vanishes if
k^{2}=o\left(n/\lambda\right)
, and in the
(\alpha,\beta)
parameter space this is equivalent to
\beta<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{4}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 1
.

The results of Proposition
5
are somewhat expected in the light of
[
RHS24b
]
, where the detection problem of a planted bipartite graph was studied. Specifically, it is shown in
[
RHS24b
]
that in the balanced case (where both sides of the bipartite graph have approximately the same number of vertices), the computational and statistical thresholds for the possibility and impossibility of detection are the same as those in Proposition
5
. By Szemerédi’s regularity lemma, any super-dense sequence of graphs can be approximated by multi-partite graphs, where the edges between different parts are regular, and the parts are of the same size. In particular, there always exists a decomposition of
\Gamma
into finitely many (possibly overleaping) approximately regular bipartite graphs
\{\Gamma^{(i)}\}_{i}
, such that at least one of the parts contains
\Theta(|e(\Gamma)|)
edges. Using a decomposition-type argument (as in Section
5.3.2
), the risk of detecting
\Gamma
can be bounded by the second moment
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L}(\Gamma^{(i)})^{2}]
of the largest component
\Gamma^{(i)}
, which, according to
[
RHS24b
]
, is bounded exactly when the condition in Proposition
5
is satisfied. Finally, using the same argument as above, the computational thresholds in Proposition
5
can be explained as well.

5.4.4

The critical regime

In this subsection, we study the regime where
p=1-o(1)
and
q=\Theta(n^{-\alpha})
, for
0<\alpha\leq 2
, and thus,
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(n^{\alpha})
. We refer to this regime as the critical regime; as will be seen later on, in this regime phase transition phenomena occur.

We remark that while our
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced decomposition argument, presented in Section
5.3.2
, is generally applicable, it is somewhat ineffective in the critical regime. Indeed, when
\Gamma
is decomposed into
M
parts, the conditions of Proposition
2
are formulated using
\lambda_{M}^{2}=(1+\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}}-1
. When
\lambda^{2}\approx n^{\alpha}
, we have
\lambda_{M}^{2}\approx n^{M^{2}\alpha}
. As
M^{2}
is expected to be large for a “good
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced approximation”, the condition of Proposition
2
becomes meaningless (since
M^{2}\alpha
will exceed
1
when
\alpha
is fixed). Despite the general applicability of our vertex cover-based lower bounds, for simplicity of representation, we will focus our investigation on
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced graphs. It is worth noting that in the bounded degree case—of particular interest due to the phase transition phenomena it exhibits—the
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanceness constraint is automatically satisfied. Finally, as will be evident in the sequel, it is quite convenient to separate the investigation into three distinct cases:
\mu(\Gamma)\geq 1
,
\mu(\Gamma)<1-\delta
, and
\mu(\Gamma)=1-o(1)
.

•

The case where
\mu(\Gamma)=1-o(1)
: We begin with the case where
\mu(\Gamma)
converge to unity from below, which is perhaps the most intriguing case, due to the emergence of a phase transition that occurs when
\alpha=1
. We have the following result.

Theorem 10
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be a
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced sequence of graphs such that
1-o(1)\leq\mu(\Gamma_{n})<1
for some
o(1)
function, and assume that
p=1-o(1)
and
q=\Theta(n^{-\alpha})
. Then, there exists a function
f(n)=o(1)
such that the following hold.

1.

Fix
0<\alpha<1
. Then, weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle|e(\Gamma)|\leq n^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}-\varepsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{\max}(\Gamma)\leq n^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}-\varepsilon},

(332)

for some
\varepsilon>0
, while strong detection is possible if

\displaystyle|e(\Gamma)|\geq n^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}+f(n)}\quad\text{or}\quad d_{\max}(\Gamma)\geq n^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}+f(n)}.

(333)

2.

Assume
q=\frac{\sigma}{n}
(in particular,
\alpha=1
), for some
\sigma>0
.

(a)

If
d_{\max}(\Gamma)=\Omega\left(|v(\Gamma)|^{\beta}\right)
, for
0<\beta\leq 1
, then weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle|e(\Gamma)|\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{\max}^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(\Gamma)\cdot\log(d_{\max}(\Gamma))\leq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\log n,

(334)

while strong detection is possible if,

\displaystyle|e(\Gamma)|\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\quad\text{or}\quad d_{\max}(\Gamma)\geq(16+\varepsilon)\log n,

(335)

for some fixed
\varepsilon>0
.

(b)

If
d_{\max}(\Gamma)=O(1)
, then:

i.

If
\sigma>2e{d^{2}}
,
then weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle|e(\Gamma)|\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon},

(336)

while strong detection is possible if

\displaystyle|e(\Gamma)|\geq n^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon},

(337)

for some
\varepsilon>0
.

ii.

If
\sigma<1
and
\mu(\Gamma)\geq 1-|v(\Gamma)|^{-\beta}
, for
0<\beta\leq 1
,
then weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle|v(\Gamma)|\leq\frac{\log\left(\frac{ed^{2}}{\sigma}\right)}{1+\varepsilon}\cdot\log n,

(338)

while strong detection is possible if,

\displaystyle|v(\Gamma)|\geq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\left(\log n\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}},

(339)

for some
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof of Theorem
10
.

The proof essentially follows by analyzing the conditions of Theorems
5
and
8
. Let
\lambda^{2}\triangleq\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(n^{\alpha})
,
k\triangleq|v(\Gamma)|
,
\tau\triangleq\tau(\Gamma)
,
d\triangleq d_{\max}(\Gamma)
, and
{\cal E}\triangleq|e(\Gamma)|
. Let us start with the case where
\alpha<1
.

1.

Assume
0<\alpha<1
. Note that the
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balances assumption implies that,

\displaystyle\tau\cdot d={\cal E}^{1+o(1)}\ll{\cal E}^{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}},

(340)

for every
\varepsilon>0
, and a sufficiently large
n
. Using the fact that
k\leq{\cal E}\ll n
, condition (
195
) in Theorem
8
implies that weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\max\left(\frac{\tau d\lambda}{n},\frac{d^{2}\lambda^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)=o(1).

(341)

By (
340
), since
\lambda^{2}=\Theta(n^{\alpha})
, the condition in (
332
) guarantees,

\displaystyle\max\left(\frac{\tau d\lambda}{n},\frac{d^{2}\lambda^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)\leq\max\left(\frac{{\cal E}^{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\lambda}{n},\frac{d^{2}\lambda^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)=O\left(n^{-\varepsilon/4}\right),

(342)

and in particular (
341
) holds. The upper bound in (
333
) follows immediately from Theorem
5
, by taking,

\displaystyle f(n)=\log_{n}\left(17\log n\right)=o(1).

(343)

2.

Assume
q=\frac{\sigma}{n}
. Let us begin with the scenario where
\Gamma
has polynomial order maximum degree, i.e.,

\displaystyle d=\Omega(k^{\beta}),

(344)

for some
\beta>0
. Assuming that
{\cal E}\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
, because,

\displaystyle\tau=\tau(\Gamma)\leq|v(\Gamma)|=|e(\Gamma)|={\cal E}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{2}=(1+o(1))\cdot\frac{n}{\sigma},

(345)

we get,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}}{\lambda^{2}d^{2}}\leq\frac{{\cal E}^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\leq n^{-\varepsilon+o(1)}=o(1).

(346)

Furthermore, the assumption in (
344
) implies that,

\displaystyle\frac{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}{n-k}\geq Ck^{\beta}=\omega(1),

(347)

where the last equality is because it is assumed that
k\to\infty
. The above two inequalities show that the condition in (
196
) holds. Thus, by (
197
) in Theorem
8
, weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}}{n-k}\cdot\left(\frac{e\cdot\lambda^{2}d^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{k-1}=o(1).

(348)

We note that since
k\leq{\cal E}
, we have
k\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
and thus,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{n}{\sigma(n-k)}\right)^{k}

(349)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{k}}\left(1+\frac{k}{n-k}\right)^{k}

(350)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{k}}\cdot\exp\overset{o(1)}{\overbrace{\left(\frac{k^{2}}{n-k}\right)}}=\frac{1+o(1)}{\sigma^{k}}.

(351)

Since
\tau\leq k
, the above shows that (
348
) holds if,

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}}{n-k}\cdot\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{\sigma}\right)^{k}=o(1),

(352)

which is equivalent to,

\displaystyle 2\log k-\log(n-k)+k(1+\log(1+\varepsilon)-\log\sigma+2\log d)\to-\infty,

(353)

and clearly holds if,

\displaystyle 2k\log d\leq 2d^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\log d\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n,

(354)

for some fixed
\varepsilon>0
. For the possibility proof, note that by Theorem
5
, as in the previous case, the count test succeeds if,

\displaystyle{\cal E}\geq n^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}+\varepsilon}.

(355)

Furthermore, by Theorem
5
the maximum degree test achieves strong detection if,

\displaystyle\min\left(\frac{d^{2}(1+o(1))}{\sigma\log n},\frac{d(1-n^{-\alpha})}{\log n}\right)=\min\left(\frac{d^{2}\lambda^{2}}{n\log n},\frac{d(p-q)}{\log n}\right)>16,

(356)

which concludes the proof.

3.

Assume
q=\frac{\sigma}{n}
, and let us now handle the case of bounded degrees, and begin with the case where,

\displaystyle\sigma>(1+\varepsilon)2ed^{2}.

(357)

A close inspection of the proof of (
195
) in Theorem
8
reveals that the constant
C
in (
195
) can be upper bounded by
2e
. Thus, assuming further that
{\cal E}\leq n^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
, by using (
345
) we obtain,

\displaystyle\frac{d\lambda}{n-k}\max\left(\tau,d\lambda\right)\leq(1+o(1))\max\left(\frac{{\cal E}^{1+\varepsilon}}{(\sigma n)^{\frac{1}{2}}},\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma}\right)\leq\frac{2e}{1+\varepsilon}.

(358)

Thus, (
195
) holds and detection is impossible. The possibility thresholds follow from Theorem
5
, as in the previous case.

Finally, we handle the case where
\sigma<1
. Here, assuming that
k\ll\sqrt{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}}{\lambda^{2}d^{2}}\leq\frac{\sigma k^{2}}{n}=o(1),

(359)

and

\displaystyle\frac{e\lambda^{2}d^{2}}{n-k}\geq Ck^{\beta}\geq(1+o(1))\frac{ed^{2}}{\sigma}>1.

(360)

In particular, the condition in (
196
) is satisfied. Similarly to the scenario of unbounded degrees, the condition in (
197
) is satisfied if,

\displaystyle 2\log k-\log(n-k)+k(1+\log(1+\varepsilon)-\log\sigma+2\log d)\to-\infty,

(361)

which holds if,

\displaystyle k\leq\frac{\log\left(\frac{ed^{2}}{\sigma}\right)}{1+\varepsilon}\cdot\log n,

(362)

for some
\varepsilon>0
. On the other hand, by Theorem
5
, the scan test succeeds if,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu\cdot\frac{d_{\mathsf{KL}}(p||q)}{\log n}>1.

(363)

Note that under the assumption that
p=1-o(1)
and
q=\frac{\sigma}{n}
, we get,

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{KL}}(p||q)=(1+o(1))\log\left(\frac{n}{\sigma}\right)=(1+o(1))\left(\log n-\log\sigma\right).

(364)

Since
\mu\geq 1-k^{-\beta}
, for some constant
\alpha
, we have,

\displaystyle\mu\cdot\frac{d_{\mathsf{KL}}(p||q)}{\log n}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-k^{-\beta}\right)\left(1+\frac{\log(\sigma^{-1})}{\log n}\right).

(365)

In particular, (
363
) holds if,

\displaystyle k>(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\left(\log n\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}},

(366)

for some fixed
\varepsilon>0
.

∎

Before we proceed to the other scenarios, let us compare Theorem
10
with the findings of
[
MST19
]
on the case where
\Gamma
is a regular tree and
q=\sigma/n
. In the situation where the maximum degree is polynomial w.r.t. the number of vertices, our bounds suggest that the statistical limits are determined by both the number of edges (as compared to
\sqrt{n}
), and the maximum degree (as compared to poly-logarithmic function of
n
). This includes the example of a planted star, studied in
[
MST19
]
, which, in our terminology, corresponds to the case where
d_{\max}(\Gamma)=|v(\Gamma)|^{\beta}
, with
\beta=1
. In this case our lower bound matches the one in
[
MST19
]
(up to a factor of
2
), and the upper bound is the same up to a factor of
\log\log d_{\max}
. We comment that the upper bound in
[
MST19
]
uses the same test (maximum degree) that we employ, but analyzed specifically for the critical regime, leading to a slightly sharper result.

In the bounded degree case, our results generalize those of
[
MST19
]
concerning
D
-regular trees. Note that bounded degree graphs are
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced, and the assumption of Theorem
10
is automatically satisfied. Our results then reveal that the phase transition observed in
[
MST19
]
is, in fact, a general phenomena. Indeed, Theorem
10
suggests that for any sequence of bounded degree graphs, there are thresholds
\underline{\sigma}(d_{\max})\leq\overline{\sigma}(d_{\max})
, such that for
\sigma<\overline{\sigma}(d_{\max})
, the barrier for detection is determined by comparing
|v(\Gamma)|
to a poly-logarithmic function of
n
, while if
\sigma>\overline{\sigma}(d_{\max})
, the barrier for detection is governed by the count test, namely,
|v(\Gamma)|=\omega(\sqrt{n})
.

We conclude this discussion by pointing out that the proof techniques used for proving the lower bounds in
[
MST19
]
, in the case where
\alpha=1
, do not generalize well to other values of
\alpha
. For example, consider the case where
\Gamma
is a path with
k
edges. Then, when
0\leq\alpha<1
, the coupling approach used in
[
MST19
]
shows that detection is impossible for
k\ll\sqrt{n}
, which is loose as compared to Theorem
10
, which states that detection is impossible as long as
k\ll n^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.

•

The case where
\mu(\Gamma)<1-\delta
:
Let
\Gamma
be a sequence of graphs such that,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu(\Gamma_{n})\triangleq\mu=1-\delta,

(367)

for some
\delta>0
. Below, we show that if
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced and satisfies (
367
), then our lower and upper bounds coincide.

Theorem 11
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of graphs satisfying (
367
). Then, for all
0\leq\alpha<\frac{1}{\mu}
, weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle|v(\Gamma)|=o\left(n^{g_{\mu}(\alpha)}\right),

(368)

while strong detection is possible if,

\displaystyle|v(\Gamma)|=\omega\left(n^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right),

(369)

where

\displaystyle g_{\mu}(\alpha)\triangleq\begin{cases}1-\frac{\alpha}{2}&0\leq\alpha\leq 1\\
\frac{1-\mu\alpha}{2(1-\mu)}&1\leq\alpha<\frac{1}{\mu}.\end{cases}

(370)

Proof of Theorem
11
.

When
0<\alpha\leq 1
, the analysis of the lower bound is exactly the same as in the proof of the first case in Theorem
10
, where we rely on the fact that if
\mu<1-\delta
, then
\Gamma
is a sequence of bounded degree graphs (see, the proof of Lemma
9
, for a detailed explanation). Hence,
\Gamma
is clearly
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced and,

\displaystyle|v(\Gamma)|\leq|e(\Gamma)|\leq v(\Gamma)\cdot d_{\max}(\Gamma).

(371)

We therefore focus on the case where
1<\alpha\leq\mu^{-1}
. Denote
k\triangleq|v(\Gamma)|
, and note that if
k\ll n^{g_{\mu}(\alpha)}
, then
k\ll\sqrt{n}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\max\left(1,\frac{n-k}{k^{2}}\right)\leq C\cdot\frac{k^{2}}{n^{\alpha}}\max\left(1,\frac{n-k}{k^{2}}\right)=o(1).

(372)

Accordingly, due to (
198
) in Theorem
8
, weak detection is impossible if,

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}}{\delta\lambda^{2}}\left(\frac{e\lambda^{2}}{\delta(n-k)}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}=o(1).

(373)

Since
\delta=\frac{1}{1-\mu}
, the above holds provided that,

\displaystyle k=o\left(n^{\frac{1-\mu\alpha}{2(1-\mu)}}\right).

(374)

Finally, the upper bound follows from the performance of the count test in Theorem
5
, combined with (
371
), which asserts that
k=\Theta(|e(\Gamma)|)
.
∎

We note that there is a gap between our lower and upper bounds in the regime where
1<\alpha<\frac{1}{\mu}
; we suspect that the upper bound is loose, and the lower bound, is tight. Indeed, we note that for
\alpha>\frac{1}{\mu}
, detection is trivial, even if
\Gamma
is finite. To see that, note that by Theorem
5
, the scan test achieves detection if,

\displaystyle\mu>(1+\varepsilon)\frac{\log n}{d_{\mathsf{KL}}(p||q)}>\frac{\log n}{\alpha\log n+C}=\frac{1}{\alpha}(1+o(1)).

(375)

We also observe that
g_{\mu}(\alpha)
in Proposition
11
approaches zero when
\alpha\to\frac{1}{\mu}
, and the lower bound becomes trivial exactly when detection becomes trivial.

•

The case where
\mu(\Gamma)\geq 1
:
Let
\mu
be defined as the limit value of
\mu(\Gamma)
, as in (
367
). Note that by the analysis of (
375
), if
\alpha>\frac{1}{\mu}
, then detection becomes trivial because the scan test is successful even if
\Gamma
is finite. We therefore assume that
0<\alpha<\frac{1}{\mu}
.

Theorem 12
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced graphs such that
\mu=\lim\mu(\Gamma_{n})<\infty
, and let
0<\alpha<\frac{1}{\mu}
. Then weak detection is impossible if

\displaystyle\max\left(|e(\Gamma)|,d_{\max}^{2}(\Gamma)\right)\leq n^{1-\alpha\mu-\varepsilon},

(376)

for some
\varepsilon>0
, while strong detection is possible if

\displaystyle|e(\Gamma)|=n^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}+f(n)}\quad\text{or}\quad d_{\max}^{2}\geq n^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}+f(n)},

(377)

where
f(n)
is and
o(1)
function.

Proof of Theorem
12
.

The proof of the upper bound follows immediately from Theorem
5
. The lower bounds are a direct consequence of (
71
) in Theorem
8
, combined with the
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanceness assumption.
∎

6

Computational Lower Bounds

This section is devoted to establishing and proving general computational lower bounds for the detection problem introduced in Section
2
. We start with a brief background on the low-degree polynomial (LDP) method; the interested reader is referred to
[
KWB22
]
, for a detailed exposition.

6.1

Background: LDP method

The LDP framework hings on the hypothesis that all polynomial-time algorithms for solving detection problems are captured/represented by low-degree polynomials. To date, there is increasing and compelling evidence supporting this conjecture. The concepts described below were developed through a fundamental sequence of works in the sum-of-squares optimization literature
[
BHK+16
,
HB18
,
HS17
,
HKP+17
]
.

We begin by outlining the fundamentals of of the LDP framework, adhering to the notations and definitions established in
[
HB18
,
KWB22
]
. Recall that any distribution
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{0}}

defines an inner product of measurable functions
f,g:\Omega_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
given by
\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{{\cal H}_{0}}=\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[f(\mathsf{G})g(\mathsf{G})]
, along with a norm defined as
\left\|f\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}=\left\langle f,f\right\rangle_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{1/2}
. Also, recall that the space
L^{2}({\cal H}_{0})
represents the Hilbert space of function
f
with
\left\|f\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}<\infty
, equipped with the above inner product. The core idea of the LDP method is to identify the “low-degree” polynomial that distinguishes
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{0}}
from
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{1}}
best in the
L^{2}
sense. To define this mathematically, let
V_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\subset L^{2}({\cal H}_{0})
denote the subspace of polynomials of degree at most
\mathsf{D}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the
\mathsf{D}
-low-degree likelihood ratio

\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}
is defined as the projection of the likelihood
\mathsf{L}_{n}
onto
V_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}
, w.r.t.
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{{\cal H}_{0}}
. Now, recall that the likelihood ratio is the optimal test to distinguish
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{0}}
from
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{1}}
, in the
L^{2}
sense. As it turns out, the
\mathsf{D}
-low-degree likelihood ratio shares a similar property
[
HS17
,
HKP+17
,
KWB22
]
.

Lemma 13

(Optimally of
\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}

[
HS17
,
HKP+17
,
KWB22
]
)
.

Consider the following optimization problem:

\displaystyle\mathrm{max}\;\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}f(\mathsf{G})\quad\mathrm{s.t.}\quad\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}f^{2}(\mathsf{G})=1,\;f\in V_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}},

(378)

Then, the unique solution
f^{\star}
for (
378
) is the
\mathsf{D}
-low degree likelihood ratio
f^{\star}=\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}/\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}
, and the value of the optimization problem is
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}
.

As we have already seen in Section
5.2
, a key characteristic of the likelihood ratio is that when
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}=O(1)
, then
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{0}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{1}}
are statistically indistinguishable (or, strong detection is impossible). The conjecture below extends this principle to the computational realm. In a nutshell, it suggests that polynomials of degree
\approx\log n
can effectively represent/cover all polynomial-time algorithms. The statement below is inspired by
[
HB18
,
HS17
,
HKP+17
]
, and
[
HB18
, Conj. 2.2.4]
. Here, we present the informal version which appears in
[
KWB22
, Conj. 1.16]
, while more formal statements can be found in, e.g.,
[
HB18
, Conj. 2.2.4]
and
[
KWB22
, Sec. 4]
.

Conjecture 2

(Low-degree conj., informal)
.

Given a sequence of probability measures
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{0}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{1}}
, if there exists
\epsilon>0
and
\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{D}(n)\geq(\log n)^{1+\epsilon}
, such that
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}
remains bounded as
n\to\infty
, then there is no polynomial-time algorithm that distinguishes
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{0}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{1}}
.

Let us derive a general expression for
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}
in our setting. To that end, we expand the likelihood ratio
\mathsf{L}_{n}
in a basis of orthogonal polynomials w.r.t.
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{0}}
. Specifically, suppose that
f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{B}:\Omega^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is an orthonormal basis for the coordinate degree-
\mathsf{D}
functions, and that
f_{0}
is the unit constant function. Then, we have,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq B}\left\langle f_{i},\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\rangle_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

(379)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq B}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}_{n}(\mathsf{G})f_{i}(\mathsf{G})\right]\right]^{2}

(380)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq B}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}f_{i}(\mathsf{G})\right]^{2},

(381)

where we have used the fact that
(\mathsf{L}_{n}-\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}})
is orthogonal to
\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{B}
. Therefore, it remains to compute
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}f_{i}(\mathsf{G})
. As we have already seen, in our setting, the corresponding basis is the Fourier character
\{\chi_{\mathsf{H}}\}_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}
defined in (
93
). As established in
[
O’D14
]
, the set
\{\chi_{\mathsf{H}}\}_{\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2},|e(\mathsf{H})|\leq\mathsf{D}}
forms an orthonormal basis for the degree-
\mathsf{D}
functions w.r.t.
\mathbb{P}_{{\cal H}_{0}}
. Thus, using (
126
), we obtain that,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|e(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}\left[\mathsf{L}\chi_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}}\right]^{2}

(382)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|e(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{1}}\left[\chi_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}}\right]^{2}

(383)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|e(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|e(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})|}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma\right]^{2}.

(384)

Furthermore, following the exact same steps in the proof of Lemma
3
, an alternative equivalent expression is,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{e(\Gamma)}{2}\\
|e(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2\left|e(\mathsf{H}^{\prime})\right|}{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathsf{H}}[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma],

(385)

where
\mathsf{H}
is an independent copy of
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
. We remark that (
385
) also follows as a corollary of Lemma
15
.

6.2

LDP analysis for planted subgraphs

Our investigation begins with the following fundamental property: all planted graphs
\Gamma
with super-logarithmic density exhibit a (conjecturally) non-empty hard region, where detection is statistically possible, yet no polynomial-time testing algorithm exists. In the second part of this section, we establish general computational lower bounds.

6.2.1

Warm up: super-logarithmic density and hardness

Before delving into the LDP analysis of our detection problem, we begin with a simple yet intriguing observation. Recall that a sequence
(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
has super-logarithmic density if

\displaystyle\mu(\Gamma_{n})=\Omega\left(\log|v(\Gamma_{n})|\right).

(386)

As mentioned in Section
3
, super-logarithmic density is a necessary condition/property for the existence of a computational-statistical gap. Conversely, for planted graphs with sub-logarithmic density, no such gap exists. In this subsection we prove that super-logarithmic density is also a sufficient condition, namely, all planted graphs with this property exhibit a computational-statistical gap. To establish this, we start with the following result.

Theorem 13
.

Let
\mathsf{D}=(\mathsf{D}_{n})_{n}
be an increasing sequence such that
\omega(1)\leq\mathsf{D}\leq o(\sqrt{n})
, and assume that
\lambda^{2}=\chi^{2}(p||q)=o(\sqrt{\mathsf{D}})
. Then, for every sequence of graphs
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
, such that,

\displaystyle|v(\Gamma)|=O\left(\left(2\lambda^{2}\mathsf{D}\right)^{-\frac{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\sqrt{n}\right),

(387)

we have
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}=O(1)
.

Proof of Theorem
13
.

Recall (
384
), and consider the following chain of inequalities,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}{\mathbb{P}}\left[v(\mathsf{H})\subseteq v(\Gamma)\right]^{2}

(388)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\left(\frac{\binom{n-|v(\mathsf{H})|}{|v(\Gamma)|-|v(\mathsf{H})|}}{\binom{n}{|v(\Gamma)|}}\right)^{2}

(389)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\left(\frac{\left(n-|v(\mathsf{H})|\right)!}{n!}\cdot\frac{|v(\Gamma)|!}{|v(\mathsf{H})|!}\right)^{2}

(390)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|}{n-|v(\mathsf{H})|}\right)^{2|v(\mathsf{H})|}.

(391)

where
(a)
follows since the vertices of a random copy of
\Gamma
are uniform over
\binom{[n]}{|v(\Gamma)|}
. Next, we upper bound the above sum by considering all possible graphs with
m
vertices and
\ell\leq\mathsf{D}
edges. Specifically, let
{\cal S}_{\ell,m,n}
denote the set of all subgraphs
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{n}{\ell}
with
|v(\mathsf{H})|=m
vertices and
\left|e(\mathsf{H})\right|=\ell
edges. The size of
{\cal S}_{\ell,m,n}
is clearly upper bounded by,

\displaystyle\left|{\cal S}_{\ell,m,n}\right|\leq\binom{n}{m}\binom{\binom{m}{2}}{\ell}\leq n^{m}\cdot\left(\frac{m^{2}}{2}\right)^{\ell}.

(392)

We also note that it must be the case that
\ell\geq\frac{m}{2}
and
m\leq 2\mathsf{D}
, and thus,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|}{n-|v(\mathsf{H})|}\right)^{2|v(\mathsf{H})|}

(393)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
1\leq|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|}{n-|v(\mathsf{H})|}\right)^{2|v(\mathsf{H})|}

(394)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}1+\sum_{m=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\sum_{\ell=m/2}^{\min\left(\binom{m}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\lambda^{2\ell}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|}{n-m}\right)^{2m}\left|{\cal S}_{\ell,m,n}\right|

(395)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{m=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\sum_{\ell=m/2}^{\min\left(\binom{m}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\lambda^{2\ell}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|}{n-m}\right)^{2m}n^{m}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{2}\right)^{\ell}

(396)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{m=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}n}{(n-m)^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{\ell=m/2}^{\min\left(\binom{m}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\left(\frac{m^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\ell}

(397)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{2\leq m\leq\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}n}{(n-\mathsf{D})^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{\ell=m/2}^{\binom{m}{2}}\left(\frac{m^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\ell}

(398)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\leq m\leq 2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}n}{(n-\mathsf{D})^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{\ell=m/2}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{m^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\ell}

(399)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}1+(1+o(1))\sum_{2\leq m\leq\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{m}\sum_{\ell=m/2}^{\binom{m}{2}}\left(\frac{2\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\ell}

(400)

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+o(1))\sum_{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\leq m\leq 2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{m}\sum_{\ell=m/2}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{m^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\ell}

(401)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}1+(1+o(1))\sum_{2\leq m\leq\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{m}\left(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2}\right)^{\binom{m}{2}}

(402)

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+o(1))\sum_{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\leq m\leq 2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{m}\left(\frac{m^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}},

(403)

where (a) follows since we only sum over graphs containing no isolated vertices, (b) is because
\mathsf{D}=o(\sqrt{n})
, and (c) is because we assume that
\lambda^{2}=\chi^{2}(p||q)=o(\sqrt{\mathsf{D}})
, and thus for
m\geq\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}
, we have that
m^{2}\lambda^{2}=\omega(1)
, which implies that,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=m/2}^{m/2+C}\left(\frac{m\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\ell}=(1+o(1))\left(\frac{m\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{C}.

(404)

Let us now handle the second term on the r.h.s. of (
403
). We have,

\displaystyle\sum_{2\leq m\leq\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{m}\left(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2}\right)^{\binom{m}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{2\leq m\leq\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{m}\left(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2}\right)^{\frac{m^{2}}{2}}

(405)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{2\leq m\leq\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\mathsf{D}^{\frac{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}}}{2}}\lambda^{2}\right)^{m}.

(406)

Clearly, the above expression is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}\left|v(\Gamma)\right|^{2}\mathsf{D}^{\frac{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}}}{2}}}{n}<1\iff\left|v(\Gamma)\right|<\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\lambda\mathsf{D}^{\frac{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}}}{4}}}.

(407)

As for the last term on the r.h.s. of (
403
), we have,

\displaystyle\sum_{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\leq m\leq 2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{m}\left(\frac{m^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\sum_{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\leq m\leq 2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{m}

(408)

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))\left(2\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{|v(\Gamma)|^{2}}{n}\right)^{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}},

(409)

where the last inequality is because we are in the regime where
|v(\Gamma)|\ll\sqrt{n}
. Combining the above, we see that (
403
) is bounded if and only if (
387
) holds, which completes the proof.

∎

We are now ready to formally state the observation mentioned at the beginning of this subsection: conditioned on the LDP conjecture, super-logarithmic density is not only a necessary condition but also a sufficient one for the existence of a computational-statistical gap.

Theorem 14
.

Assume that
\chi^{2}(p||q)=O(1)
,
\mu(\Gamma)\gg\log|v(\Gamma)|
, and that Conjecture
2
holds. Then, there is an
\Omega(1)
function and an
\omega(1)
function
f_{\Gamma}
, which depends on
\Gamma
, such that whenever,

\Omega(1)\cdot\log n\leq\mu(\Gamma)\leq f_{\Gamma}(n)\cdot\log n,

(410)

while detection is statistically possible, a polynomial-time algorithm that achieves strong detection does not exist.

Proof.

Using Theorem
13
and Conjecture
2
, we deduce that there is no polynomial-time algorithm the distinguishes between
{\cal H}_{0}
and
{\cal H}_{1}
if for some arbitrarily small
\varepsilon
we have,

\displaystyle\log|v(\Gamma)|

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{(2\lambda^{2}(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon})^{\frac{\sqrt{(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon}}}{2\sqrt{2}}}}\right)

(411)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log n-\frac{\sqrt{(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon}}}{2\sqrt{2}}\cdot\log\left(2\lambda^{2}(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon}\right)

(412)

\displaystyle=\frac{\log n}{2}\cdot(1+o(1)),

(413)

and if stated in terms of
\mu(\Gamma)
,

\displaystyle\mu(\Gamma)\leq(1+o(1))\frac{\log n}{2}\cdot\overset{f_{\Gamma}(n)=\omega(1)}{\overbrace{\left(\frac{\mu(\Gamma)}{\log|v(\Gamma)|}\right)}}=\omega(1)\cdot\log n.

(414)

On the other hand, by Theorem
5
, detection is possible (via the scan test) if
\mu(\Gamma)=\Omega(\log n)
.
∎

6.2.2

General LDP analysis

In this subsection, we establish tighter computational lower bounds than those derived in the previous subsection. To that end, we adopt the same approach and reasoning as in the proof of our statistical lower bounds in Section
5
, focusing in particular on the representation in (
385
). We have the following result.

Theorem 15
.

Let
\mathsf{D}=(\mathsf{D}_{n})_{n}
be an increasing sequence such that
\omega(1)\leq\mathsf{D}\leq o(n)
and let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be a sequence.

1.

Assume that
\lambda^{2}=\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(1)
. If,

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\max(\chi^{2}(p||q),1)\cdot 2\mathsf{D}^{2}\right)^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma)d_{\max}(\Gamma),d^{2}_{\max}(\Gamma)\right)}{n-|v(\Gamma)|}\leq C,

(415)

then
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}=O(1)
.

2.

Assume that
\chi^{2}(p||q)=o(D^{-2})
. If,

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\chi^{2}(p||q)}\cdot\mathsf{D}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma)d_{\max}(\Gamma),d^{2}_{\max}(\Gamma)\sqrt{\chi^{2}(p||q)}\cdot\mathsf{D}\right)}{n-|v(\Gamma)|}\leq C,

(416)

then
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}=O(1)
.

Proof of Theorem
15
.

We start by proving (
415
). To that end, we recall (
385
). Our approach is reminiscent to the proof of Theorem
6
, but now, the maximal number of vertices is
2\mathsf{D}
, and we run only on graphs with at most
\mathsf{D}
edges, the maximal number of edges
j
in a subgraph with
\ell
vertices is dominated by
\binom{\ell}{2}\wedge\mathsf{D}
. Hence, using the same arguments as in (
5.3.1
)–(
5.3.1
) and Lemma
, we obtain,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{e(\Gamma)}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2\left|\mathsf{H}\right|}{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathsf{H}}[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma]

(417)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|\lambda^{2j}\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(418)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}|\lambda^{2j}\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(419)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\tau^{m}(ed)^{\ell-m}\binom{\binom{\ell}{2}}{j}\lambda^{2j}\frac{(2\tau)^{m}d^{\ell-m}}{(n-k)^{\ell}}

(420)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\binom{\binom{\ell}{2}}{j}\lambda^{2j}

(421)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\binom{\ell}{2}^{j}\lambda^{2j}

(422)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\right)^{m}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}

(423)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\min\left(\frac{\ell^{2}}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\right)^{m},

(424)

where in the fourth inequality we have used the Hardy-Ramanujan formula, and the sixth inequality is because we assume without loss of generality that
\lambda^{2}>1
; otherwise, we may replace
\lambda^{2}
by
1
and proceed with the analysis. We now separate our analysis into two complementary cases, starting with the case where,

\displaystyle\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\geq 1+\alpha,

(425)

for some fixed
\alpha>0
, independent of
n
. In this case, we have,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\min\left(\frac{\ell^{2}}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\right)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}

(426)

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\Bigg[\sum_{\ell=2}^{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e}\tau d}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\frac{\ell^{2}}{2}}

(427)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e}\tau d}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\Bigg[\sum_{\ell=2}^{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e}\tau d}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\left(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2}\right)^{\frac{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\ell}{2}}

(428)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\left(\frac{(2\mathsf{D})^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e}\tau d}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\Bigg[\sum_{\ell=2}^{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e}\tau d(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}

(429)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\left(\frac{(2\mathsf{D})^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e}\tau d}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\Bigg].

(430)

Now, the first sum on the l.h.s. of (
430
) converges when

\displaystyle\frac{\tau d(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-k}<C,

(431)

for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
. A necessary condition for the second term on the r.h.s. of (
430
) to converge is that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e}\tau d}{n-k}<1-\alpha,

(432)

for some fixed
\alpha>0
, in which case, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)2\tau d}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\leq C_{\alpha}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e}\tau d}{n-k}\right)^{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}}.

(433)

Hence, the second term on the r.h.s. of (
430
) is bounded if,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau d\left(2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}\right)^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-k}<C,

(434)

for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
. In the limit
\mathsf{D}\to\infty
, the condition in (
431
) is satisfied if (
434
) holds, which implies that
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}
is bounded provided that (
434
) holds. Next, we move to the complementary case where,

\displaystyle\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\leq 1+o(1),

(435)

for some
o(1)
function. Here, for a sufficiently large
n
, the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
430
) is dominated by
\left(1+\varepsilon\right)^{\ell}
, and thus,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\min\left(\frac{\ell^{2}}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}.

(436)

Accordingly, if we repeat the exact same steps as in the first case above, we finally obtain that,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\Bigg[\sum_{\ell=2}^{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}ed^{2}(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}

(437)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\left(\frac{(2\mathsf{D})^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\Bigg],

which converges if,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}(2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-k}<C,

(438)

for a sufficiently small
C>0
. We conclude by noting that (
425
) holds exactly when the condition (
434
) dominates the condition (
438
) (up to a fixed multiplicative constant, and therefore (
415
) sufficient condition.

Finally, we prove (
416
). This is quite similar to the above analysis, and our derivations proceed from (
422
) as follows,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\binom{\ell}{2}^{j}\lambda^{2j}

(439)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\left(2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}\right)^{j}

(440)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}}\right)^{m}\frac{(2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2})^{\ell-m}}{1-2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}}

(441)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)ed^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}}\right)^{m},

(442)

where third inequality follows from the assumption that
\lambda^{2}=o(\mathsf{D}^{-2})
. Accordingly, in a similar fashion to the analysis of the previous case, we see that if,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}}\geq 1+\alpha,

(443)

for some positive
\alpha>0
, then (
442
) is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau d\mathsf{D}\lambda}{n-k}<C,

(444)

for a sufficiently small constant
C
. Otherwise, if,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}}{ed^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}}\leq 1+o(1),

(445)

for some
o(1)
function, then (
442
) is bounded provided that,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}}{n-k}<C.

(446)

Combining the above conditions, we obtain (
416
), which concludes the proof.
∎

6.3

Reduction to the vertex cover-degree balanced case

There is a gap between the computational lower bound in Theorem
15
and the performance of the efficient algorithms (i.e., the count and maximum degree tests) in Theorem
5
, which disappear if
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{{vcd}}
-balanced. We remedy this and prove that Theorem
5
is tight using a similar strategy as in Subsection
5.3.2
. We start with the following variant of Proposition
2
.

Proposition 6
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of graphs, and assume that each can be decomposed into a set of edge-disjoint graphs, i.e.,
\Gamma=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{M}\Gamma_{\ell}
.

1.

If
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(1)
, and there exists a constant
C
such that,

\displaystyle\max_{\ell=1,\dots,M}\frac{\left(\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{2}\cdot M^{4}\mathsf{D}^{2}\right)^{\sqrt{M^{2}\mathsf{D}/2}}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell}),d^{2}_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})\right)}{n-|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|}\leq C,

(447)

where
\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{2}\triangleq\max(1,2eM^{2}\lambda^{2},\lambda_{M}^{2},C_{\lambda}(e\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}})
,
\lambda_{M}^{2}=(1-\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}}-1
, and
C_{\lambda}>0
is a universal constant, then
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}=O(1)
.

2.

If
\chi^{2}(p||q)=o(D^{-2})
, and there exists a constant
C
such that,

\displaystyle\max_{\ell=1,\dots,M}\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}\mathsf{D}\cdot\max\left(\tau(\Gamma_{\ell})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell}),d^{2}_{\max}(\Gamma_{\ell})\bar{\lambda}_{M}\mathsf{D}\right)}{n-|v(\Gamma_{\ell})|}\leq C,

(448)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{2}\triangleq\max\left(\lambda_{M}^{2},eM^{4}\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2},(eM^{2}\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}},\mathsf{D}M^{2}\left(eM^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}\right)^{M^{2}}\left(M^{4}\mathsf{D}\right)^{M^{4}}\right),

(449)

then
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}=O(1)
.

To prove Proposition
6
we need the following generalization of Theorem
15
, which provides the conditions under which the truncated moment generating function of the intersection of
two arbitrary
subgraphs is bounded.

Lemma 14
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}=(\Gamma_{1,n})_{n}
and
\Gamma_{2}=(\Gamma_{2,n})_{n}
be two sequences of graphs. Then, there exists a universal constant
C
such that:

1.

If
\chi^{2}(p||q)=\Theta(1)
, and

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\max(\lambda^{2},1)\cdot 2\mathsf{D}^{2}\right)^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}\max\left(\sqrt{\tau(\Gamma_{1})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{1})\tau(\Gamma_{2})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{2})},d_{\max}(\Gamma_{1})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{2})\right)}{n-\min\left(|v(\Gamma_{1})|,|v(\Gamma_{2})|\right)}\leq C,

(450)

then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2}|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]=O(1),

(451)

where the expectation is taken w.r.t.
\Gamma_{2}\sim\mathsf{Unif}({\cal S}_{\Gamma_{2}})
and
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
is fixed copy of
\Gamma_{1}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
.

2.

If
\chi^{2}(p||q)=o(D^{-2})
, and

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\chi^{2}(p||q)}\cdot\mathsf{D}\cdot\max\left(\sqrt{\tau(\Gamma_{1})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{1})\tau(\Gamma_{2})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{2})},d_{\max}(\Gamma_{1})d_{\max}(\Gamma_{2})\sqrt{\chi^{2}(p||q)}\cdot\mathsf{D}\right)}{n-\min\left(|v(\Gamma_{1})|,|v(\Gamma_{2})|\right)}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\leq C,

(452)

then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{2}|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]=O(1).

(453)

Before we prove Lemma
14
, we will require the following simple generalization of Lemma
3
.

Lemma 15
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two arbitrary (perhaps different) subgraphs of
{\cal K}_{n}
and
\mathsf{D}
be an integer. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min\left(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|\right)}\binom{|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(454)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right],

(455)

where
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
is a fixed arbitrary copy of
\Gamma_{1}
in
{\cal K}_{n}
, the summation is over graphs containing no isolated vertices, and the probabilities and the expectation are taken w.r.t. two independent random copies
\Gamma_{i}\sim\mathsf{Unif}({\cal S}_{\Gamma_{i}})
, for
i=1,2
.

Note that in the limit
\mathsf{D}\to\infty
, the binomial sum on the l.h.s. of (
101
) converges to
(1+\lambda^{2})^{|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}
, and we obtain Lemma
3
.

Proof of Lemma
15
.

We follow the exact same steps as in the proof of Lemma
3
, and start with the analysis of the term on the r.h.s. of (
454
). We have,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]

(456)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]

(457)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}

(458)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=e\left(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}\right)\right]\sum_{i=0}^{\min\left(\left|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\right|,\mathsf{D}\right)}\binom{|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}|}{i}\lambda^{2i}

(459)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min\left(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|\right)}\binom{|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right],

(460)

which proves the first equality in (
454
). For the second equality in (
455
), recall that as explained in the proof of Lemma
3
, for any graph
\mathsf{H^{\prime}}\subseteq{\cal K}_{n}
, if
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to some subgraph
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
, then
\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\right]=\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H^{\prime}}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\right]\neq 0
. If
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
is not isomorphic to any subgraph of
\Gamma_{1}
, then the probability above is clearly zero. We define an equivalence relation on subgraphs of
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
, where two subgraphs are considered equivalent if and only if they are isomorphic. Let
[\mathsf{H}]
denote the equivalence class of a subgraph
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}
, and let
{\cal P}
be the set of all equivalence classes corresponding to graphs with at most
\mathsf{D}
edges.
Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}_{\mathsf{D}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\cong\mathsf{H}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}^{\prime}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(461)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}_{\mathsf{D}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\cong\mathsf{H}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(462)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}_{\mathsf{D}}}|{\cal S}_{\mathsf{H}}|\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\right]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(463)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}_{\mathsf{D}}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\cal N}(\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{1})\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(464)

\displaystyle=\sum_{[\mathsf{H}]\in{\cal P}_{\mathsf{D}}}\sum_{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\in[\mathsf{H}]}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(465)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right].

(466)

which completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
14
.

The proof follows the exact same steps as in the proof of Lemma
7
with the modifications as in the proof of Theorem
15
. Let us follow the notation used in the proof of Lemma
7
, where
\tau_{i}=\tau(\Gamma_{i})
,
d_{i}=d_{\max}(\Gamma_{i})
,
\mu_{i}=\mu(\Gamma_{i})
,
k_{i}=|v(\Gamma_{i})|
,
\bar{k}=\min(k_{1},k_{2})
, and
\bar{\mu}=\min(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. Let
{\cal S}(m,\ell,j)(\Gamma_{1})
be the set of subgraphs of
\Gamma_{1}
with
m
connected components,
\ell
vertices, and
j
edges. We repeat the steps as in the proof of Lemma
7
, but now, the maximal number of vertices is
2\mathsf{D}
, and we consider only graphs with at most
\mathsf{D}
edges. Furthermore, note that the maximal number of edges
j
in a subgraph with
\ell
vertices is upper bounded by
\binom{\ell}{2}\wedge\mathsf{D}
.
We begin with the case where
\lambda^{2}=\Theta(1)
. Using Lemmas
4
,
, and
15
, and following similar derivations as in the proof of Lemma
7
, we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min\left(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|\right)}\binom{|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]

(467)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2|\mathsf{H}|}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\right]

(468)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}|{\cal S}_{m,\ell,j}(\Gamma_{1})|\lambda^{2j}\frac{(2\tau_{2})^{m}d_{2}^{\ell-m}}{(n-\bar{k})^{\ell}}

(469)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\left|\mathsf{Par}(\ell,m)\right|\tau_{1}^{m}(ed_{1})^{\ell-m}\binom{\binom{\ell}{2}}{j}\lambda^{2j}\frac{(2\tau_{2})^{m}d_{2}^{\ell-m}}{(n-\bar{k})^{\ell}}

(470)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\binom{\binom{\ell}{2}}{j}\lambda^{2j}

(471)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\binom{\ell}{2}^{j}\lambda^{2j}

(472)

\displaystyle{\leq}1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{m}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}

(473)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\min\left(\frac{\ell^{2}}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{m},

(474)

where the last inequality is because we assume, without loss of generality, that
\lambda^{2}>1
; otherwise, we can replace
\lambda^{2}
by
1
and proceed with the same analysis. We separate the following analysis into to complementary cases. In the first, we assume that,

\displaystyle\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\geq 1+\alpha

(475)

for some fixed
\alpha>0
, independent of
n
. In this case, we have,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\min\left(\frac{\ell^{2}}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}

(476)

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\Bigg[\sum_{\ell=2}^{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{2}}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\frac{\ell^{2}}{2}}

(477)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{2}}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\Bigg[\sum_{\ell=2}^{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{2}}(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}

(478)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\left(\frac{(2\mathsf{D})^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{2}}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\Bigg].

(479)

The first sum on the l.h.s. of (
479
) converges when,

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{2}}(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-\bar{k}}<C,

(480)

for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
. A necessary condition for the second term on the r.h.s. of (
479
) to converge is that,

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{2}}}{n-\bar{k}}<1-\alpha

(481)

for some fixed
\alpha>0
, in which case, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{1}}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\leq C_{\alpha}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2e\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{1}}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}}.

(482)

Hence, the second term on the r.h.s. of (
479
) is bounded if,

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}d_{1}d_{2}}(2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-\bar{k}}<C,

(483)

for a sufficiently small constant
C>0
. In the limit
\mathsf{D}\to\infty
, the condition in (
480
) is satisfied if (
483
) holds, which implies that
\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}
is bounded provided that (
431
) holds. Next, we move to the complementary case where,

\displaystyle\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\leq 1+o(1),

(484)

for some
o(1)
function. Here, for a sufficiently large
n
, the inner sum on the r.h.s. of (
479
) is dominated by
\left(1+\varepsilon\right)^{\ell}
, and thus,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{\ell^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\min\left(\frac{\ell^{2}}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}.

(485)

Accordingly, if we repeat the exact same steps as in the first case above, we finally obtain that,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\Bigg[\sum_{\ell=2}^{\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}ed_{1}d_{2}(\mathsf{D}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}

(486)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\left(\frac{(2\mathsf{D})^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)^{\mathsf{D}}\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}}\right\rfloor+1}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-k}\right)^{\ell}\Bigg],

which converges if,

\displaystyle\frac{d_{1}d_{2}(2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2})^{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}/2}}}{n-k}<C,

(487)

for a sufficiently small
C>0
. Combining the above conditions, we obtain (
450
), which concludes the proof for the case where
\lambda^{2}=\Theta(1)
.

Next, we handle the case where
\lambda^{2}=o(D^{-2})
in a similar fashion. We proceed our analysis from (
472
), and get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min\left(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|\right)}\binom{|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]

(488)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\binom{\ell}{2}^{j}\lambda^{2j}

(489)

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{m}\sum_{j=\ell-m}^{\min\left(\binom{\ell}{2},\mathsf{D}\right)}\left(2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}\right)^{j}

(490)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)ed_{1}d_{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}}\right)^{m}\left(2\mathsf{D}^{2}\lambda^{2}\right)^{\ell-m}

(491)

\displaystyle=1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)2ed_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lfloor\ell/2\right\rfloor}\left(\frac{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}}\right)^{m},

(492)

where
(a)
follows from the assumption that
\lambda^{2}=o(\mathsf{D}^{-2})
. Now, consider the case where

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}{ed_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}}>1+\alpha,

(493)

for some fixed constant
\alpha
. In this case, the inner sum in the r.h.s. of (
492
) is dominated by the last term, and we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{2}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min\left(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|\right)}\binom{|e(\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]\leq 1+C_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\sum_{\ell=2}^{2\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)\lambda\mathsf{D}\sqrt{ed_{1}d_{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}{n-\bar{k}}\right)^{\ell},

(494)

which is bounded if

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda\mathsf{D}\sqrt{d_{1}d_{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}{n-\bar{k}}<C,

(495)

for some universal constant
C
. Similarly if the l.h.s. of (
493
) is upper bounded by
1+o(1)
, then the inner sum in the r.h.s. of is dominated by
(1+\varepsilon)^{\ell}
, and, accordingly, (
492
) is bounded if,

\displaystyle\frac{d_{1}d_{2}\lambda^{2}\mathsf{D}^{2}}{n-\bar{k}}\leq C.

(496)

Combining the conditions in (
495
) and (
496
), we obtain (
453
).
∎

Before proving Proposition
6
, we note that while the argument of Lemma
7
extends relatively easily to the low-degree scenario, generalizing the Hölder’s inequality argument used in Proposition
2
is significantly more technically involved. Specifically, for the statistical lower bounds, the second moment
\mathbb{E}_{{\cal H}_{0}}[\mathsf{L}(\mathsf{G})^{2}]
is given by
\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}[\exp(c\cdot|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime}|))]
. Substituting the decomposition of
\Gamma
into
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{M}
, and applying Hölder’s inequality allows us to analyze each crossing term
\mathbb{E}[\exp(c_{M}\cdot|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|))]
, separately. In the low-degree case, on the other hand, this is not the case anymore. Indeed, as seen from Lemma
15
, the low-degree likelihood’s second moment is given by,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|)}\binom{|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]

(497)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},\sum_{i,j}|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime})|)}\binom{\sum_{i,j}|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime})|)}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right].

(498)

At first glance, it is rather unclear whether the above expression can be bounded using a product of expressions of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime})|)}\binom{|e(\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime})|)}{m}\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{2m}\right],

(499)

for some “well-behaved”
\bar{\lambda}_{M}=f(\lambda^{2},M,\mathsf{D})
, as in the statistical lower bound case. In Proposition
6
, we provide a positive answer to this question, which forms the main argument in the proof.

Proof of Proposition
6
.

First, we note that by Lemma
15
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n,\leq\mathsf{D}}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{H}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}\\
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\mathsf{D}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{2\left|\mathsf{H}\right|}{\mathbb{P}}_{\Gamma}\left[\mathsf{H}\subseteq\Gamma\right]^{2}

(500)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|)}\binom{|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right],

(501)

where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is an independent random copy of
\Gamma
, both drawn uniformly at random over
{\cal S}_{\Gamma}
. While all the expectations throughout the following analysis are with respect to the distribution of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
, we drop the
\Gamma\perp\!\!\!\perp\Gamma^{\prime}
notation from the expectations for the sake of readability. Consider an edge-disjoint decomposition
\Gamma=\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma_{i}
. We next show that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|)}\binom{|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\sum_{m_{i,j}=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right].

(502)

To that end, let us rewrite the term on the r.h.s. of (
502
) as follows. We let

\displaystyle{\cal J}_{\mathsf{D}}\triangleq\left\{(m_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{M}\in[D]^{M^{2}}:0\leq m_{i,j}\leq\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|),\forall 1\leq i,j\leq M\right\}

(503)

Below, for brevity, we will use the following shorthand notation for multiple sums,

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1,1}=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}|)}\sum_{m_{1,2}=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}|)}\ldots\sum_{m_{M,M}=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{M}\cap\Gamma_{M}^{\prime}|)}\equiv\sum_{(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\mathsf{D}}}.

(504)

Then, clearly,

\displaystyle\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m}\lambda^{2m}=\sum_{(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\mathsf{D}}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}}\lambda^{2m_{i,j}}.

(505)

Now, we note that,

\displaystyle\sum_{(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\mathsf{D}}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}}\lambda^{2m_{i,j}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sum_{m=0}^{\sum_{i,j\in[M]}\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)}\lambda^{2m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\mathsf{D}}\\
\sum_{i,j}m_{i,j}=m\end{subarray}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}}.

(506)

We next show that (
506
) is lower bounded by the sum on the l.h.s. of (
502
). First, we notice that,

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in[M]}\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)\geq\min\left(\mathsf{D},\sum_{i,j\in[M]}|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|\right)=\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime}|),

(507)

and thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\mathsf{D}}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}}\lambda^{2m_{i,j}}\geq\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime}|)}\lambda^{2m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\mathsf{D}}\\
\sum_{i,j}m_{i,j}=m\end{subarray}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}}.

(508)

We recall Vandermonde’s identity,

\displaystyle\binom{|\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime}|}{m}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\infty}\\
\sum_{i,j}m_{i,j}=m\end{subarray}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}},

(509)

where

\displaystyle{\cal J}_{\infty}\triangleq\left\{(m_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{M}:0\leq m_{i,j}\leq|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|,\forall 1\leq i,j\leq M\right\}.

(510)

Then, the sum on the l.h.s. of (
502
) can be rewritten as,

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|)}\binom{|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|}{m}\lambda^{2m}=\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|e(\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime})|)}\lambda^{2m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\infty}\\
\sum_{i,j}m_{i,j}=m\end{subarray}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}}.

(511)

Thus, in light of (
508
) and (
511
), in order to prove (
502
), it suffices to show that,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\mathsf{D}}\\
\sum_{i,j}m_{i,j}=m\end{subarray}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}}\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m_{i,j})_{i,j}\in{\cal J}_{\infty}\\
\sum_{i,j}m_{i,j}=m\end{subarray}}\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m_{i,j}}.

(512)

Indeed, the key here is the range of
m
, namely,
0\leq m\leq\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime}|)=\min(\mathsf{D},\sum_{i,j\in[M]}|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)
. Let
{\cal Q}
denote the pairs
(i,j)
for which
\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)=|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|
, and then
{\cal Q}^{c}=[M]^{2}\setminus{\cal Q}
. Note that pairs
(i,j)\in{\cal Q}
, the summations that corresponds to
(i,j)
have the sum range in both sides of (
512
), and as so, we may focus on
{\cal Q}^{c}
. In this case,
\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)=\mathsf{D}
, and thus, the range of these pairs
(i,j)
in the summation on the l.h.s. of (
512
) is
0\leq m_{i,j}\leq\mathsf{D}
, which contained in the range of those on the r.h.s. of (
512
), which is
0\leq m_{i,j}\leq|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|
. While at first sight, this might seem problematic, since for both terms in (
512
) we have

\displaystyle 0\leq\sum_{i,j}m_{i,j}=m\leq\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma\cap\Gamma^{\prime}|)=\min\bigg(\mathsf{D},\sum_{i,j\in[M]}|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|\bigg)=\mathsf{D},

(513)

it must be that
0\leq m_{i,j}\leq\mathsf{D}
for the term on the r.h.s. of (
512
) as well. Thus, (
512
) holds, and in fact, with equality. Combining (
505
), (
506
), (
508
), (
509
), and (
512
), proves (
502
).

Next, applying Hölder’s inequality on (
502
), we get that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]\leq\prod_{i,j\in[M]}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]^{M^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{M^{2}}}.

(514)

We now analyze the inner expectation on the r.h.s. of (
514
), for any given pair
(i,j)
. Let
\varphi\in[0,1]
be defined as
\varphi\triangleq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{1+\lambda^{2}}
, and as so
\lambda^{2}=\frac{\varphi}{1-\varphi}
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m}\lambda^{2m}

\displaystyle=(1-\varphi)^{-|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}\cdot\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m}\varphi^{m}(1-\varphi)^{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|-m}

(515)

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda^{2})^{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{B}\leq\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)\right],

(516)

where
\mathsf{B}\triangleq\mathsf{Binomial}(|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|,\varphi)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{m=0}^{\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)}\binom{|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}{m}\lambda^{2m}\right]^{M^{2}}=(1+\lambda^{2})^{M^{2}|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|}\cdot\left(\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{B}\leq\min(\mathsf{D},|\Gamma_{i}\cap\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}|)\right]\right)^{M^{2}}.

(517)

Now, let
\left\{\mathsf{B}_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell=1}^{M^{2}}
be
M^{2}
independent random copies of
\mathsf{B}
